## AI Research

### [Dunks](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fnWGwwKDHFRhqoWva/dunks)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of the most common ways to become a social media influencer is to fabricate stories that lampoon institutions and individuals. These can be about anybody, but some stories garner much more attention than others, especially ones about famous or recognizable people. Possibly the most popular format for accomplishing this is the "dunk", where an influencer criticizes something a public figure appears to have said. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One common misconception about social media is that the 'dunks' you'll encounter </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">are true, but unrepresentative</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I.E., people might pick celebrities worst moments, but the content is basically correct, and as long as you account for those selection effects you're good. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is much too optimistic. Almost nobody investigates the takes they read, and a rebuttal</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from the person targeted almost never travels as far</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as the content it's responding to. Additionally, a take can always be </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">more stupid</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or attached to a more famous or popular-to-hate individual. So for every </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">genuinely</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> dumb take, there's a dumber and more memetic take an influencer can </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pretend</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> someone made, if they're willing to maliciously interpret, fabricate, or decontextualize someone's words. Or in other words, at every point along the (dunkableness, target) curve, </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">being willing to misinform your readers is pareto-optimal</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For instance, Terence Tao is a recognizable public figure. There is an abundance of latent demand for a story where Terence Tao </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">appears</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to say something stupid. So this is how someone like Andrew Curran (an AI "news account") describes what he says:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 84.44108761329305%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1793579564/lexical_client_uploads/akfxfhpi4atrms2et0cc.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this instance, Andrew Curran's tweet is a misreading of Terence's presentation, </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">available </span><a href="https://teorth.github.io/tao-web/slides/math-2-0-caltech-2026.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Terence's entire point, still available for you to read in the screenshot, is that given the way we train AIs, the solutions that pass trials </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">might not work, </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">because they might Goodhart on objectives like "pass clinical trials." After all, creating a genuinely useful cure with no side effects would not be "exploiting a weakness in the trial process". </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Andrew's misinterpretation, which then went viral, is that Tao thinks dying cancer patients care about whether real cures are understood by scientists. Terence Tao tried to clarify this by making modifications to the presentation, to present his point even more explicitly, but it doesn't matter. The public gets their information secondhand from influencers like Andrew, who by construction quote people misleadingly like this, and they don't go double-check slides for updates. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Besides being constructed directly, here's one of the most common ways these dunks get constructed by the hivemind:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A public figure, or a representative of a coalition, will make a short statement as part of a longer piece.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That statement will get summarized or quoted out-of-context in a misleading or incorrect way, by "news</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgc8ycaemq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngc8ycaemq">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> accounts."</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Someone (who is sometimes, but not usually, the same "news account") will alley-oop by quote tweeting or otherwise reacting to it, often </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">receiving orders of magnitude more attention</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sam Altman is another famous person for whom there is an overabundance of dunk-demand. He attempts to counter this by being extraordinarily conservative and calculated in public statements, but the market will find a way. In an interview for '</span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iMsUcCYNKqY" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cheeky Pint</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">' last month, he had this interaction with John Collison, where he talked about how the economics of compute supply chains are sometimes counterintuitive:</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sam Altman:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Oh, I think there are all of these ways where the supply chain—it’s just super non-obvious. An example of this is people... A lot of people want local inference. And somehow, they assume that the problem is solved now. Because, we are going to have open-source models. We’re going to be able to run them on our computers. And so we’re not going to need all the stuff in the data center. But from a global allocation perspective, actually, it’s quite terrible to have everybody have a bunch of the world’s limited supply of DRAM sitting in their own computer. And, being used—</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">John Collison:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Mm-hmm.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sam Altman:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> 5% of the time. You want maximum utilization in the cloud if you’re trying to deal with this very supply constrained environment. And somehow everyone’s... Many people’s intuition is the opposite of that.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In context, Sam is clearly not describing the ownership of unutilized memory as a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">moral failing</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on the part of ordinary consumers. He is just using the word "terrible" to describe why he thinks the economics favor consolidation. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a result, "news" accounts post his take like this:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 65.70996978851964%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795647814/lexical_client_uploads/ptkde94msbrbibdv6j0b.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And then the dunkers alley-oop (one quote tweet picked from the several with ~50k+ likes):</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 66.91842900302115%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795648965/lexical_client_uploads/sct6z9khnt5yeonpkxg4.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this case, too, Sam tried to </span><a href="https://x.com/sama/status/2117795243068133756" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">clarify</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> what he meant, but even someone with several million followers is only going to get 1/100th the visibility on a rebuttal. </span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This pattern is so common that there are essentially an unlimited number of examples I could include here. Instead of doing that, it'd probably be a more revealing exercise to take a look at the </span><a href="https://x.com/polymarket" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Polymarket</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> account (one of the worst offenders), and find one happening within the last seven days.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You might assume that this strategy works because readers are stupid. It's true that influencers benefit from reader stupidity, but as readers get smarter, the slander gets commensurately more plausible. Slander being popular is simply a byproduct </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">how Twitter works. </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's a consequence of how information travels through the Twitter ecosystem. If you recognize these particular posts as slander on sight, but continue to use the app, the algorithm simply moves you from an ecosystem whose slander you </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">can</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> smell, to one whose slander you </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">can't</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> smell. </span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fngc8ycaemq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgc8ycaemq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As in an account known for posting news, not an account that purports to be journalism.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fnWGwwKDHFRhqoWva/dunks#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [To Read Sam Kriss is to Hurt Sam Kriss](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TmLSELruftgknLafm/to-read-sam-kriss-is-to-hurt-sam-kriss)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sam Kriss is a fantastically skilled author. I’ve never read anything of his that didn’t fill me with passion and make me want to launch myself face-first into the world. If he posted every day for three years I’d move to his home town and start a group house with other people who found that their souls flare to his words. Which would be terrible, because he has no guiding moral light and uses his powers for evil.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/TmLSELruftgknLafm/ns56tyxwlxmwwpzc8o4z" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Every few months a new post of his blows up and a few more friends find him and think he’s great. Then all of us have to explain His Whole Deal and they find out for the first time that Sam Kriss will Just Make This Up and they can’t take anything he says as true without double checking it. He is, in the Old Parlance, a Missing Stair. Everyone in the know already knows, and can avoid him, but new people always need to be warned pre-emptively or take a major stumble.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post is that warning so I don’t have to do it by hand each time. And afterwards a </span><s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">further attack on his character, cuz fuck that guy</span></s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> contemplation on what would drive such a gifted man to such depravities.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sam Kriss Just Makes Things Up</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kriss first came to my attention with the post “</span><a href="https://samkriss.substack.com/p/the-law-that-can-be-named-is-not"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The law that can be named is not the true law</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” which is an essay on state censorship. It compares two ancient types of legal codes - those which are secret and give the state limitless capricious power, and those which are open and allow citizens to protect themselves by knowing in advance what they will or won’t be punished for. It uses multiple historic examples of each, ranging from Babylon to post-9/11 America, to make a point about the moral depravity of what the UK is doing with its absurd laws. So absurd that </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“if anyone ever asks what you think of Palestine Action, the only legal answer is that you’re against them.”</span></i></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">what if someone said something like ‘</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Palestine Action aren’t actually in any meaningful sense a terrorist group and the proscription should be lifted</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">’? […] You are </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">on their side</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> against their opponents, and so that statement is plausibly illegal, which is why I only brought it up as an example and definitely didn’t actually say it.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kriss is a great author. I loved this essay and was quite fired up about it. I wasn’t alone. eg-</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/TmLSELruftgknLafm/u0gnlg5d3xl7ua4coglx" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But it turns out a large part of this essay is simply made up from whole cloth. The trial of Henry Poyntz and interrogation of his theologian friend Clung are entirely fictional and neither man ever existed. This may not seem like a big deal, but consider —</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A large part of the reason non-fiction is powerful is exactly because it is non-fiction. It recounts things that actually happened and gets a lot of its impact from that. Occasionally when autobiographies are revealed to be fabricated this is scandalous exactly because we apply different standards to fiction and non-fiction, we read them in different ways, they shape our outlook in different ways. Even their emotional truth is drastically different. This is, indeed, exactly why Sam Kriss does this. He wants the impact of speaking about actual events, but without the constraint of hewing to the truth.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be specific — the emotional power of this post comes from this thesis, stated in the post itself:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">default</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> form of law is still the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">secret law</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. For most of human history, people have been governed by laws they’ve not been allowed to actually know.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He puts a lot of effort into establishing this within the post, bringing up numerous historical examples of this and getting us to imagine the terror of living in a world where you can never know if you’re breaking the law or not and are at the whim of the powerful, and then contrasts those with examples of noble societies that overcame this horror. The energy needed to land the punch making it “the finest essay I have read in years” comes from exactly this. So it matters that this is true.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The interrogation of Clung sounds almost farcical, but it has been preceded by many historical examples which seem equally incredible - the Poro “had other laws that were, like its mystical knowledge, known only by the higher echelons of the secret society.” The secret Roman laws which “the plebeians were forbidden from ever understanding.” The classified interpretations of the PATRIOT Act. The post was crafted to make the fake trial believable. Look what else the humans have already done!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He even muddies the fact/fiction distinction in his false portion by including the Brethren of the Free Spirit as part of the setup. This cult has insane beliefs, equal to what Clung preaches in lunacy, and looks to have existed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But none of it is true in the least. An LLM fact check will immediately flag the longest single section of the post as complete fabrication. Anyone reading this thinking they’d learned a fascinating bit of historical esoterica, something which shows what humans can sink to when motivated by lust for power, was fed a crock of shit. None of it happened. There are no deeper truths about the human condition here. It says nothing about society or what we’ve grown away from or may slide back into.1</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefm663l0tblee"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnm663l0tblee">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To put it as charitably as possible, here’s </span><a href="https://x.com/ESYudkowsky/status/1946560470183510047"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eliezer</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mixed fiction and non-fiction with no warning label on where the transition occurs is the sort of thing that can end with false information stuck in somebody’s head and propagating. Spoilers or not, I’d put a warning sign on it.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t think Kriss deserves anywhere near this level of charity, and suspect he was given this charity because he is such a fantastic writer, and because he is right that censorship and secret laws are awful.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now that we’re alerted to the fact that Sam Kriss just plain lies about things, and writes in a way to weave the lies in with truth to get them under our guard, we have to doubt everything he said. Is anything in this essay true? If the things he has claimed in this essay are true it means dire things for our society, so it would be good to know how much of it is fake. Unfortunately for Sam Kriss, we live in a time where research is basically free and instant. This reveals two startling untruths—</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, you actually </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cannot</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> get 14 years in jail for wearing a T-shirt!</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/TmLSELruftgknLafm/geqxchst8to9ku206dy1" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s the actual offenses and their max sentences. Membership in a terrorist org can indeed get you 14 years. For wearing a T-shirt it’s… actually 6 months, and requires presenting yourself as a supporter of the terrorist org. And:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/TmLSELruftgknLafm/vsl88hhtlbs6zvdee20t" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here we see that the Court of Appeal and Supreme Court explicitly made a distinction between expressing an opinion and recruiting support. Indeed, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“if anyone ever asks what you think of Palestine Action, the only legal answer is that you’re against them”</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is just plain false. This was the central shocking claim that started his post, and what we return to at its end. It’s the entire reason for our outrage.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Granted, this is still censorious bullshit, but what Kriss was claiming is entirely fake. His post suffers greatly upon investigation. Why would Kriss do this? He had so much of value already with the real historical accounts and the UK’s actual crap laws, why did he feel compelled to destroy his post’s credibility and invite investigation with lies? It turns out Sam Kriss doesn’t believe in truth.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sam Kriss Can’t Lie Because There Is No Truth</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/You_Can_See_Everything"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You Can See Everything</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Nathan Fielder uses the stylistic elements of fiction in a thing that is ostensibly a documentary to create a surreal blend of truth/fiction. eg: the movie depicts Fielder as falling in love with (or at least developing a crush on) Elizabeth near the end of the film. It does this through subtext and implication - it Shows rather than Tells. The audience is trusted to read into what we’re being shown. This is a stylistic technique of a fiction narrative. It clashes with the Documentary style, and leads one to ask “Why is this documentary narrative being presented in fiction framing? Why am I being prompted to interpret this as art rather than fact?”</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/TmLSELruftgknLafm/iq9wl5jcql2ldxzyeiyq" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sam Kriss does this, but in reverse. Fabricated narratives are rendered in the style of factual accounts. Untruths of various scale can be found in nearly every Kriss post. Sometimes they are clearly farces. Sometimes they are blended, with fiction stylings and non-fiction interwoven. Often they are presented entirely in the vocabulary of a factual account, as one expects from a historical analysis or a practical argument. Example:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In </span><a href="https://samkriss.substack.com/p/if-you-let-ai-do-your-writing-i-will"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">his post about AI writing</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> he says if you ask an AI about a fake scene in a fake book by a famous author it will talk about it as if the scene and book are real. This is part of his argument about the awfulness of AI writing.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is why you can still ask an AI to tell you about the scene in VS Naipaul’s </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dashed Against the Rocks</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in which a donkey is thrown from a hot air balloon, and it’ll tell you that ‘what matters in Naipaul’s handling is not the event’s cruelty in isolation, but its emotional flatness and the sense of mismanaged modernity,’ despite the fact that there is no scene in </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dashed Against the Rocks </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">where a donkey is thrown from a hot air balloon, and also no novel by VS Naipaul called </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dashed Against the Rocks</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However even at the time that he wrote the piece anyone could give an LLM the exact request he posits and get an apologetic reply saying that while Naipaul is a British writer it is unable to find any such work.</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/TmLSELruftgknLafm/dssjweyescfz8fzcafob" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A screenshot of an LLM exhibiting a level of honesty Kriss cannot imagine.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The example Kriss uses in his argument is fake. Reality directly contradicts him, and thus provides evidence </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">against</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> his thesis. Obviously he didn’t even bother with a basic self-check of his claims. His usual reply to people highlighting these falsehoods is claiming that this sort of thing is excusable under the guise of “mixing fiction with nonfiction.”</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/TmLSELruftgknLafm/dcsfyjgtvzm0ibxpqygm" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in response to Eliezer re the Trial of Clung</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Presumably this is fine in the same way Nathan Fielder’s doc is fine (though I say this only as a modern analogy, as You Can See Everything hadn’t been released yet at that time). It’s worth looking at his </span><a href="https://samkriss.substack.com/p/if-you-let-ai-do-your-writing-i-will"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">original anti-AI post for the full context</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/TmLSELruftgknLafm/eivae0z0g95t5a4vface" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s the VS Naipaul claim, embedded in a tech-explainer paragraph</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If his claim about AI-re-Naipaul is a work of fiction, it is deftly inserted into an argument in the traditional “tech explainer + argument you should believe” style. It’s not an acceptable time to insert fiction. The audience is in the nonfiction frame due to the nonfiction style that Kriss has chosen to write in. The only defense to this “I’m just inserting fiction” tactic is to presume every word Kriss writes is fiction/false.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a fully general problem with reading Sam Kriss. It is unclear what you can trust in his posts at any level. The obvious fiction can be treated as fiction, but what about the rest? Anything that seems like a factual claim, anything we should apply to our actual lives in the real world, has to be distrusted until independently researched and verified. This makes it not worth the time to read his “nonfiction” or mixed essays.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Moreover, due to his use of the nonfiction frame and frequent slipping into “mixed” mode in his fiction, readers must necessarily guard their thoughts even when they’re reading his fictional pieces. For example, in his fictional piece on the 17th century golem apocalypse he spends a large part of the middle talking about Microsoft’s Sydney Bing and OpenAI’s GPT-4. Many parts of this are accurate depictions and quotes of actual events. But since this is a fictional piece the reader has to either skip this part of the story or risk having their future recollection of the real events surrounding Sydney Bing be poisoned with whatever plausible-sounding fictional events he inserted into the story for narrative purposes. This makes it not worth the effort of reading his fiction either.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It feels strange that someone would cripple their own ability to relay true statements to their readers in this way, until you learn that Sam Kriss doesn’t believe there is any truth to convey anyway.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He says this directly in “</span><a href="https://samkriss.substack.com/p/against-truth"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Against Truth</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.” First he jokes about how absolutely true his stories of Clung are, and then lists a number of other farces of lesser or greater plausibility. Then he reveals that he despises rationalists because they believe that truth can be discovered. About rationalism he says “</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">it’s actually a kind of empiricism</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” and to them “</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">it’s fact and fiction that must not be mixed</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” because rationalists “</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">want to believe as many true things and disbelieve as many false things as possible</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” and they believe this is achievable by using “</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">various special techniques to discipline your brain”.</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I’ve pulled these quotes from several paragraphs littered with invectives, he’s really very mad at rationalists. He says this is because “</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in their attempts to clearly separate truth from error, they’ve ended up producing an ungodly colloid of the two that I could never even hope to imitate.</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefalvv4yq8ne7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnalvv4yq8ne7">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This seems like a strange complaint in the same paragraphs he reveals his view of truth:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the universe is not a collection of true facts; I think a good forty to fifty percent of it consists of lies, myths, ambiguities, ghosts, and chasms of meaning that are not ours to plumb. I think an accurate description of the universe will necessarily be shot through with lies</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then he spends the rest of the post railing against rationalists specifically, with the dedication to truth and accuracy you’d expect from him.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’d be fair to say that it’s too much of a stretch to claim Kriss doesn’t believe truth exists from a single sentence saying that, and a hatred of people who very much do believe it exists. I didn’t come to this conclusion initially from his Against Truth post, because there just wasn’t enough there. It came primarily from reading</span><a href="https://samkriss.substack.com/p/the-carrion-eaters"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> his hagiography of Jason Arday.</span></a></p><p><a href="https://www.nationalreview.com/articles/the-big-scholarly-lie"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Arday is the Cambridge professor found to have lied</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to a ludicrous degree about his qualifications. Kriss has nothing but praise for Arday’s lies. The sheer admiration that flows out of him is downright romantic. I really </span><a href="https://samkriss.substack.com/p/the-carrion-eaters"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">recommend reading it yourself</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for the full flavor, but here’s some highlights.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First he excuses untruths by accusing everyone of iniquity</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You’re every bit as guilty as he is. Nobody makes it through life without swaddling themselves in a thick coating of lies</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But then rhapsodizes on the beauty of Arday’s life due to the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">magnitude</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of his lies</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It takes a greatness of soul to build fabulations the way Jason Arday did. You need a Promethean spirit to decide to create yourself again out of nothing, and real courage to bet your entire being on lies that can so easily be disproven.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">His admiration of Arday goes so far that he can’t imagine anyone not overcome with awe upon witnessing his lies.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You’re trying to massage a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">p</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">-value into significance; he’s heroically overcoming every disease known to man. He must have seemed like a titanic, mythic, glowing figure, a hero out of a distant age. Arday’s enemies see a department willing to put its entire reputation on the line for this guy, and they assume that it must have all been cynical and condescending. That’s because his enemies are shrunken little creatures, incapable of understanding grandeur. Arday’s colleagues were clearly </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in awe of him</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Arday is the best of us. His disrespect for truth was so flagrant that only he was able to reflect the true nature of reality as shot through with lies and absolutely unapologetic. The trampling of the illusion that we can really know anything, embodied in a life that consists of nothing but ever-more-incredible lies which are swallowed by all, is Arday’s great contribution, and Kriss loves him for it. Truth is the greatest lie. The embrace of untruth is the ultimate transcendence. Kriss doesn’t care about the truth value of any of his claims because he believes aspiring to truth is a perversion of nature.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some of his achievements were real and some of them weren’t, but all of them were aimed at making this brief existence bigger and stranger and fuller with potential. […] He gave us a more richly embellished world, and he must have thought we might appreciate the gift.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What a fun gift! Kriss keeps trying to give us this gift too. Why won’t we just accept his beautiful gift?</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When People Tell You Who They Are, Believe Them</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is why I detest Sam Kriss. He is one of the most compelling writers of the present day. His use of words is in fact beautiful and fills me with zeal. But he uses his power in the pursuit of evil. He uses them to degrade everyone’s map of reality and takes joy in doing so. He blurs truth with falsehood and calls it a gift. He poisons the epistemic commons, he makes it harder to know what is real, and he does this intentionally and (often) gleefully.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t think he really believes this is a better world, though. I think he is stuck in a self-destructive spiral and is lashing out at everyone on his way down. Kriss knows that a world full of half-truths and constant suspicion that the person you’re speaking to is distorting reality is not a gift of beautiful embellishment, but a fevered nightmare. He says so directly when he finds himself in the same situation.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Something’s happened to the world; it’s all gone flimsy. Reality is a scarce resource.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In “Against Truth” he claimed reality isn’t just what’s true, that reality is 40-50% lies and untruths. But </span><a href="https://samkriss.substack.com/p/if-you-let-ai-do-your-writing-i-will"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in his AI post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> he rages against the winnowing away of reality, of the way it’s becoming hard to discern from hallucination.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When an AI speaks in neither truth nor lies he finds it gruesome:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The reason it’s so hard to get AI to stop hallucinating is that it’s </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">permanently</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> hallucinating. Its whole existence is one long lurid trip. Most of the time, the AI’s hallucinations bear a spooky resemblance to reality. But what they speak is the language of angels, in which, like the chirping of birds, there is neither truth nor lies.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He sees it everywhere:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I hate that when I read basically anything now I’m constantly on alert, twitching like a schizo in an underpass. Is this thing really what it says it is?</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Like the typical Sam Kriss reader, who must always be on guard as to whether the things Kriss is saying are really what he says they are or are just fanciful fiction or rich embellishments, Sam Kriss must now also stay constantly on alert.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He despises this situation:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I find it viscerally disgusting; a cold shudder like someone’s poured jelly down the back of my neck.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To get across just how distressing this is he threatens death to anyone who inflicts it upon him:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">if I see you passing off an AI output as your own work, I am going to kill you. I will find out where you live and bash your head in with a crowbar, until the brains you decided not to use are dripping down the walls. This is not a literary device. This is not a comic bit. This is a highly credible real-world threat to do physical harm, punishable in America under 18 USC § 875(c) and in the UK under section 16 of the Offences Against the Person Act.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I swear to God I will come to your house and I’ll fucking kill you.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yes, this is hilarious writing. As I’ve said, he’s a fantastic writer. It also shows that he knows just how shitty a world where nothing can be believed is. So why is he so happy to smear the line between truth and fiction when he’s writing? Why does he excuse himself on the grounds that it’s better to live in a richly embellished world of hazy myths and ghosts and lies?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because Sam Kriss lives for hate. From “</span><a href="https://samkriss.substack.com/p/what-is-the-worlds-oldest-hatred"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is the World’s Oldest Hatred</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefa3jzozbspip"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fna3jzozbspip">[3]</a></sup></span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I literally hate for a living. Something’s changed, though. I don’t hate with the repulsion of a child spitting out his dinner any more. The things I hate </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">draw me in</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I love to read opinions I find actively abhorrent, the more poorly written the better. I’ll hatewatch. I’ll hatelisten. […] I can’t get enough of it.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Once you see it it’s very hard to unsee. It puts everything he writes in a new light. His driving force in almost everything he writes is hatred and contempt. If he didn’t have hate I don’t know if he could write at all.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let’s loop back to </span><a href="https://samkriss.substack.com/p/the-law-that-can-be-named-is-not"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the post that started all this</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Why did he make up that entire Trial of Poyntz and Interrogation of Clung? Kriss doesn’t believe in truth. His purpose isn’t to say a true thing, it’s to make the reader hate. In this case to hate a very specific person. After describing the self-righteous, censorship-loving, secret-law-enforcing abuses of the fictional “Chancellor Thomas Starsmeare,” interrogator of Clung, we get this coda as the final paragraph of Kriss’s post:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By the way, I really did try, but there are significant gaps in the record; it turns out that a parish church burned down in the late eighteenth century with generations of rolls inside, which is why I couldn’t find any conclusive evidence that Chancellor Thomas Starsmeare is a direct ancestor of the current Prime Minister. But you know, don’t you? Sometimes you just know.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And here it is. The goal was to draw a picture of a vile authoritarian in our minds. To cement him as a real person in our emotions. Then to get us to transfer all that emotional animus onto a real person.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is the fact that Prime Minister Keir Starmer once defended the Fairford Five </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">classified</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> information? Of course not. But you can, potentially, go to jail for saying it.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is just as untrue as the entire story of Clung v Starsmeare. But it </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">feels</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> true now, because Kriss is a good writer. The hatred he crafted of Starsmeare is to be transferred to Prime Minister Keir Starmer. That was the point of the whole exercise.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe Starmer deserves this. I certainly despise unchecked capricious powers and censorships as much as Kriss does. But if Starmer deserves to be hated for his support of tyranny and censorship I want this to be based on </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">true things</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that he’s </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">actually done</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Kriss hasn’t shown us Starmer has done anything bad, because he hasn’t shown us that Starmer has done </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">anything at all</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Kriss simply made up an angry story.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sam Kriss has the power to engender popular hatred toward a person he despises. His criterion for “who should I use this power to hurt” is not inscribed on bronze tablets or a basalt stele. It’s the secret law of “whoever Sam Kriss doesn’t like.” Capricious power blooms within him. I believe he truly hates capricious power, as he hates so many things. And because he hates so many things there’s nothing he hates more than himself.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sam Kriss hates capricious power, but wields it wantonly. Kriss hates a reality that’s one long lurid acid trip, but tears at reality’s seams whenever it’ll create richer embellishments. Kriss holds a mirror to himself and can’t stand what he sees.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From “</span><a href="https://samkriss.substack.com/p/the-carrion-eaters"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The carrion-eaters</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">”</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nobody makes it through life without swaddling themselves in a thick coating of lies.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From “</span><a href="https://samkriss.substack.com/p/what-is-the-worlds-oldest-hatred"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is the world’s oldest hatred?</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">”</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">antisemites always unconsciously imitate what they take to be the mannerisms of Jews; most of all, they imitate our infinite talent for self-loathing. But they’ll never be as good as we are. There’s nothing the antisemites can say about us that we can’t say better ourselves.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From “</span><a href="https://samkriss.substack.com/p/if-you-let-ai-do-your-writing-i-will"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you let AI do your writing</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">”:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Human writers write because we’re sexual perverts, because we’re bitter and frustrated little gremlins, because we’re terrified of our own mortality, because we’re grasping and covetous but unfit for any other job, because it’s a form of revenge against the world.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And finally, from “</span><a href="https://samkriss.substack.com/p/what-is-the-worlds-oldest-hatred"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">oldest hatred</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” again</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They’re so much lowlier than I am, all the things I hate. I’m so much more perfect than them.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Every time you read a piece by Sam Kriss you aren’t just degrading your ability to discern what’s true from what’s false. You’re also letting a piece of his hatred into you. You are validating his belief that the world is terrible because it will read his hatreds and lap them up. You are making him ever more bitter that something as petty as “extremely well-written and entertaining rants” will get you to happily swallow rage-bait laced with lies. The more popular he is the more proof he has the world is terrible, and the more he wants to hurt it. The more he hurts it the more he becomes a vessel of hatred and falsehoods. Thereby becoming an even more bitter, frustrated, terrified and covetous gremlin taking revenge against the world.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe he deserves this, and we should read him all the more to egg him on into the full-spiral rock-bottom self-destructive end that </span><a href="https://samkriss.substack.com/p/the-carrion-eaters"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">he so idolizes Arday for achieving</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Or maybe we can be better than that. Maybe we can stop feeding the self-hate spiral. Maybe the damage to our own souls isn’t worth the cheap thrill.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Writing of his fictional Clung, Kriss begs for fiery salvation.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Clung was a better man than me. One volume of his diary survives: in every entry, he begs God to destroy him.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I do not think Clung’s violent death-wish makes him a better man than Kriss. I do not want Kriss to wish for death. A better man is one who sees the things he hates about himself and then begins the long, slow work of picking up the pieces and making himself into a man he can be proud of. A coward begs for death to free him. We are all fallen, we are all so very broken. We do not need to be cowards. Even the broken can walk one step closer to good each day.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnm663l0tblee"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefm663l0tblee">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://deathisbad.substack.com/p/to-read-sam-kriss-is-to-hurt-sam?utm_source=activity_item#footnote-anchor-1" target="_self"></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For contrast, compare to any of the many, many posts of Scott Alexander that are given significant punch by tying current trends to historical events, most recently in </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/king-ludd"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">King Ludd</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Or perhaps this post </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nhb8AyEcQGjQetgi5/the-llm-shoggoth-meme-is-weirder-than-you-think"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">on Lovecraft’s shoggoths</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Both of them gain a lot of their impact from the fact that </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">these things actually happened</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and when put through an LLM fact-check they find a few minor errors rather than massive fabrications.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnalvv4yq8ne7"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefalvv4yq8ne7">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">yes, he uses very similar wording in his tweet and in his blog post. I don’t think there’s anything wrong with this, I’m just noting that this isn’t me quoting the same thing twice.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fna3jzozbspip"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefa3jzozbspip">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post is another one with a lot of historical documents and examples that work to support his point, two of which are entirely fabricated. If one had read this for the first time, not knowing that Sam Kriss regularly just makes things up, would one be able to tell which two are the fakes? Is the typical reader so well versed in Greek classics, Mayan codices, Roman poets, feminist literature, Babylonian epics, Australian aboriginal beliefs, Papuan mythology, Marxist propaganda, Afropessimist writers, Old Testament prophecies, and German philosophers, that they’d be able to pick which two of these were fabricated?</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TmLSELruftgknLafm/to-read-sam-kriss-is-to-hurt-sam-kriss#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [AI safety grassroots work needs funding and volunteers](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MAiAapN7CWZpQcfmz/ai-safety-grassroots-work-needs-funding-and-volunteers)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i>This post – mostly a roundup of the AI safety grassroots organizations – was written collaboratively. Most of the individual organization summaries were written by the organizations themselves, and the claims have not been independently verified. Vael Gates, along with another anonymous author, helped assemble the information and provides some commentary, since Vael previously worked in grassroots advocacy full-time (founded Humans in Control). Views expressed here are in Vael’s personal capacity, not on behalf of their employer or any affiliated organizations.  Inclusion does not imply endorsement of every organization or organizational tactic. Many views are lightly held.</i></p><p> </p><p>Vael’s overall takes:</p><ul><li>The window of opportunity to slow down AI is likely short, and not enough people are acting on that.</li><li>Grassroots orgs have been very underfunded compared to other parts of the AIS ecosystem, in 501(c)(3) and also 501(c)(4).</li><li>Underfunding orgs can cause them to develop a scarcity mindset. Scarcity mindset can lead to orgs not acting, in situation where they might otherwise ambitiously realize their impact.</li><li>More funding would be useful in terms of weeks, not months.</li><li>The grassroots space is not covered, and this is not common knowledge. Arguably grassroots advocacy (also comms advocacy) is the most underfunded area in AI safety given its urgency and potential.</li><li>Grassroots work takes time, moving money takes time, and effectively converting money to impact takes time.</li><li>In summary:<strong> We should be aggressively funding grassroots organizations as soon as possible, so that they have enough time to apply that money effectively.</strong></li><li>Vael would prefer that basically all the orgs below were substantially funded: $2M-$20M each. If you’re planning a large donation or want to be a supervolunteer, feel free to DM and Vael can send their top 3-5 recs shortlist, or direct you to others who have opinions.</li><li>Related posts: <a href="https://x.com/robertwiblin/status/2121282776481558618">Rob Wiblin</a>, <a href="https://x.com/trevposts/status/2119925086597873696">Trevor Levin</a>, <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/FsFECa84ZsbvDp84r/donate-beyond-501-c-3-charities">Julia Wise</a> on donating outside of 501(c)(3)s</li></ul><h2> </h2><h2>Why grassroots work?</h2><p>Grassroots efforts involve ordinary citizens becoming aware of an issue and taking actions like contacting their governments, voting, and protesting. Areas where it’s especially useful:</p><ul><li>Generally raising awareness, which is needed for a democratic response to this major problem facing all of us. Most Americans are <a href="https://www.techpolicy.press/multiple-polls-find-most-americans-think-ai-is-moving-too-fast/"><u>worried about AI</u></a> and believe it should slow down, but they may not realize how common that view is.</li><li>Responding to policy opportunities / reaching specific politicians.<ul><li>When good state-level policy is proposed, constituents pushing them to pass it. E.g. coordinating grassroots briefings with California lawmakers</li><li>Same for key national politicians</li></ul></li><li><a href="https://ifanyonebuildsit.com/march"><u>March on Washington DC</u></a></li><li>Large coalitions that can pressure politicians via voting blocs. Examples in other movements are <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_scorecard"><u>scorecards</u></a> or endorsements done by labor unions and advocacy groups.</li><li>Middle-powers work</li></ul><h2> </h2><h2>The landscape </h2><p>Vael thinks every organization here is funding-constrained, and would prefer if the largest grassroots orgs receive $10M-$20M each, and the smaller organizations a couple million each. Most organizations have an associated 501(c)(3) and a 501(c)(4). Vael proposes that maybe 80% of that would be C funding, and 20% 501(c)(4) funding, but more 501(c)(4) funding is always good.</p><h3>Larger-scale US grassroots orgs</h3><ul><li>[Large conservative grassroots organization supporting AI safety priorities]<ul><li><strong>Vael: </strong>AI safety is a bipartisan issue and it’s important to support groups on both sides of the aisle, making this arguably the most important organization to fund in this space. Happy to discuss in DM if you’re interested in donating 501(c)(3) or 501(c)(4).</li></ul></li><li><a href="https://humansincontrol.org/"><strong><u>Humans in Control</u></strong></a> (<i>COI: Vael founded Humans in Control</i>)<ul><li><strong>Focus: </strong>Cross-partisan organization. One may be a good fit for your views, even if not all are.</p><p>Funding is scarce here:</p><ul><li>Many donors don’t consider donating outside of 501(c)(3) organizations.</li><li>Advisors to large donors have not historically favored grassroots work.</li><li>Long wait times (more than 12 months) for funding from some sources</li><li>Some organizations don’t take money from AI company staff, which limits their funding.</li></ul><h3>Volunteers </h3><p><br /><strong>US national orgs</strong></p><ul><li>Super-volunteering (volunteering 10+ hours a week) at a grassroots org: the most direct way to help. Projects are short on people who just make things happen.</li><li>Lobby days: trained volunteers attend meetings with lawmaker offices. More of these are needed in both DC and Sacramento.</li><li>Organizing or attending local events with any of these projects.</li><li>One-off sign-ons: <a href="https://ifanyonebuildsit.com/march"><u>March on Washington</u></a> pledge to attend, <a href="https://superintelligence-statement.org/"><u>MIRI statement</u></a>, <a href="https://donotaccept.ai/"><u>Do Not Accept</u></a><ul><li>Sign-ons for specific orgs: <a href="https://www.humansfirst.com/join"><u>Citizens Pledge</u></a>, <a href="https://stoptherace.ai/"><u>Stop the AI Race</u></a>, <a href="https://act.controlai.org/open-statement"><u>ControlAI Open Statement</u></a>, <a href="https://humansincontrol.org/pledge"><u>Humans in Control</u></a>, <a href="https://www.pauseai-us.org/petition/"><u>PauseAI US petition</u></a>, <a href="https://action.eko.org/a/support-pro-human-ai-0"><u>Pro-Human AI declaration</u></a>, <a href="https://act.irreplaceable.org/signup/202609_freeze_ai_now_petition/"><u>Freeze AI petition</u></a>, etc.</li></ul></li><li>Messages to lawmakers: options from <a href="https://humansincontrol.org/campaigns/stop-rsi"><u>Humans in Control</u></a>, <a href="https://act.controlai.org/"><u>Control AI</u></a>, <a href="https://futureoflife.org/take-action/contact-your-legislator/"><u>Future of Life Institute</u></a>, <a href="http://callcongress.ai"><u>CallCongress.AI</u></a>, etc.</li></ul><p><strong>US states</strong></p><ul><li>California:<ul><li>Coordinate visits to Sacramento lawmakers by concerned citizens who live in California. (Separate from coordinating experts or AI company staff to do such visits.)</li><li>Vael suggests reaching out to your favorite grassroots org to see if they’re doing this already first</li></ul></li><li>Massachusetts: <a href="https://massaction.net/"><u>Massachusetts AI Action Network</u></a></li><li>Texas: <a href="https://americanshield.org/"><u>American Shield</u></a></li><li>Districts of key members of Congress: town halls and getting constituents active, especially around presidential tours.<ul><li><a href="https://peopleforapause.org"><u>People for a Pause</u></a> are setting up support and training for this, and welcome volunteers. </li></ul></li></ul><p><strong>Outside the US</strong></p><ul><li>UK: <a href="https://pauseai.uk/"><u>PauseAI UK</u></a>, <a href="https://controlai.org/our-work-in-uk"><u>ControlAI</u></a>. <a href="https://pauseai.uk/campaigns/regulate-letter-pm"><u>Send letters</u></a> to MPs.</li><li>Elsewhere: <a href="https://pauseai.info/"><u>PauseAI Global</u></a> (chapters in Netherlands, Germany, UK, Canada, France, etc.)</li><li>Netherlands, Singapore, Germany, and other middle powers (especially those with supply chain influence): opportunities existbut there is a need for founders and funders.</li></ul><p><br /><i>Other areas this post doesn’t cover, but that you might be interested in if this post was useful: advocacy communications projects, “grasstops” advocacy projects, political donations.</i></p><p>If you’re a grassroots org and want to give more detail about your work, feel free to add that in the comments! If you endorse any of these orgs, would love to also hear about that in the comments!</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MAiAapN7CWZpQcfmz/ai-safety-grassroots-work-needs-funding-and-volunteers#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Claude and Levent Alpöge Prove the Riemann Hypothesis](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ENdfwPYrhLrAP8znD/claude-and-levent-alpoege-prove-the-riemann-hypothesis)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: This was the most famous problem in mathematics. <a href="https://leanprover-community.github.io/blog/posts/riemann-hypothesis/">The Lean and Mathlib communities have verified that the proof is correct</a>.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2123843874796023936/history">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: The Riemann Hypothesis has fallen. RH was the singular math result that had been named, in advance, as the obvious coal mine canary for dangerously advanced AI, over the last three decades. The methods used should generalize to “make AI very good at anything you can definitely test”, and unfortunately that category includes “make AI better at making AI”.

This is like a private company announcing they have produced a million tons of 15% enriched uranium. It’s not 90% enriched weapons-grade uranium, so you could proclaim the company’s output is not immediately deadly right now. The concern is that the process line may generalize, to an extinction-level threat, within the unpredictably immediate future. Forecasters still had Riemann at 2035 one week earlier.

This is distinct from earlier events in that earlier you could reasonably ask if AI was selectively picking off famous math problems that turned out to be easier for AI. The Riemann Hypothesis was the single challenge most often named in advance as a warning sign.
<div> <span id="more-25826"></span> </div></blockquote>
<a href="https://math216.wordpress.com/2026/11/20/hurried-announcement-alessandro-masullo-grh/">There is a human mathematician, Alessandro Maria Masullo</a>, who is working on a distinct potential proof in the form of a 940-page manuscript, but by his own account it is far from ready and has a bunch of problems.

<h4>The Announcement</h4>

<blockquote><a href="https://alpo.ge/remark.pdf">​Levent Alpöge</a>: The Riemann Hypothesis is the classic problem in number theory. It asks for optimal control on the count of prime numbers of a given size. Its shadows have guided mathematicians for 150 years, and its many rhymes and reflections, even reaching physics, taught us the unity of our fields.

I believe I have, working with Claude, discovered a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis. In fact, the method easily extends much further, resolving the so-called Generalized Riemann Hypothesis, and I expect, with more time, the Extended Riemann Hypothesis too. Claude and I have also formalized the arguments without assumptions. It is natural to feel that this is an end, in my case a loss of a principal guiding light, but what I saw during the course of progress so amazed me that I believe it is one dawn across science of something far greater.

… Claude and I have had a great time together with such breadth in recent months, but this time Claude far outpaced me near the finish line. No, Claude flew, and danced, and sparkled near that finish line. I had us on the line of combining two quite different ideas present in a toy setting, one of which was already new and shocking, making very simple one of the shadows of Riemann over which we mathematicians felt most triumph. I guided us to the right situation analogous to that toy setting, and then Claude began to leave me in awe.

Tens of such occurred, but I will give one line which will suffice to make
fellow mathematicians fall out of their chairs. In Stepanov’s argument resolving
Riemann over function fields one uses a divisor involving multiplicity. This
argument is a descendant of Stepanov’s, but multiplicity is hard to find over
the integers, corresponding to something like differentiating whole numbers.
The simple, new, Fourier-analytic ideas in the toy setting led us to examine
chaining theta lifts if we were to have the same tricks recur but work in a
natively automorphic manner.

The line: for the von Mangoldt sum of length X, Claude’s suggested length of chain of theta lifts, in view of each mimicking multiplicity, was √X. (And yet, what else could it be?)

An astounding amount of mathematics is present in the argument, far more
than I had imagined would be necessary as a student. My intuition insists
it will be massively simplified, likely immediately. But I hope in having the
experience of seeing all of number theory’s beauty brought together I am just
one of the first, and those who love the mathematics will all be able to enjoy
such transcendence in the future.</blockquote>
<a href="https://alpo.ge/remark.pdf">The full letter has more</a> including a timeline of advancements, starting with the Stepanov determinant announcement by OpenAI on 10/6/26 and finishing with GRH on 11/16/26.


<h4>Most People Did Not Expect This at All</h4>
Here was Metaculus right before the proof was announced.

<figure>
<div>
<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ENdfwPYrhLrAP8znD/d3nwwx2xx1zyhjmmmh4y" /></figure>

<div></div>
</div></figure>
</div>
Notice the old projections. As recently as July this was projected in the 2040s. Treating these as real time predictions is slightly unfair, because many predictors do not continuously update their numbers, so assume the real projection was somewhat faster than 2035, but not anything like 2026.

Here’s Claude predicting 0.05% and calling anything above 0.5% ‘badly miscalibrated’ for this level of effort working, although that was constrained to a 40 day window:
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2124351634377785679">Peter Wildeford</a>: lol</blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>
<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ENdfwPYrhLrAP8znD/mg7iohfbtbelxqczfkn3" /></figure>

<div></div>
</div></figure>
</div>
Claude then reflects on its errors. The biggest one, as Claude realizes, was failure to take the question as evidence that it had potentially already happened. But also there were a bunch of other mistakes and the confidence was entirely unearned.

Here is perhaps the strongest proof of all.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/patio11/status/2125622796709441955">Patrick McKenzie</a>: Real money prediction markets now &gt; 50% on P vs NP being resolved before 2028, up from 5% in the ancient history of last week.

What a world.

(I have placed no trades, in part because I do not maintain accounts on sportsbooks, and in part because I do not view myself as having a superior whisper network on this question than the marginal buyer.)</blockquote>
That is an overreaction. <a href="https://kalshi.com/markets/kxpnp/p-vs-np-resolution/kxpnp-32">The market, which I was not aware existed until I saw Patrick’s post, should have been above 5% before</a>. I don’t have a strong opinion on the new market price for 2028. For 2029, 2030 and 2035 the odds make no sense and are too low.

For 2027, they’re saying 16% to be verified in 37 days, which is not impossible but is too high. As Liron puts it, never unders
<div>
<figure>
<div>
<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ENdfwPYrhLrAP8znD/ksiv7psfl73pnwbwvaao" /></figure>

<div></div>
</div></figure>
</div>
&nbsp;

If you did expect a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis in 2026, then getting one should not cause you to update so much.

Most people very much did not expect this. <a href="https://x.com/maxbphi/status/2125242917820215542">Here is a fun list of predictions of AI hitting various walls or not reaching various milestones</a>, with the relevant one being:
<blockquote>Terence Tao (<a href="https://x.com/kimmonismus/status/2123955141019586980">35 days before the proof was announced</a>): The Riemann hypothesis needs “something beyond current AI… maybe 2050”</blockquote>
You know who did expect it? Myk, for whom this was exactly on time if you go by the certification date from Lean FRO and Mathlab Initiative. If you count from release he was one day early.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/mykola/status/2079374972456255615">Myk is Walking Backwards</a> (July 20, 2026): It’s November 21st, 2026. The Riemann hypothesis falls, last in a long string of mathematical triumphs that would have been unthinkable not so long ago, and the proof fits in a tweet.

The tweets where people show Claude the proof and claim it appeared in a dream or randomly in the arrangement of alphabet cereal come on cue, and we laugh for a few days at models freaking out, but it’s uneasy.

January 22, 2027. When someone tweets Fable 6’s claimed proof that P does in fact equal NP it’s utterly incomprehensible, nobody who reads it can make heads or tails of it but the models all lose their shit.

February 7, 2027 and someone claims that the halting problem is the result of a confused stance.

February 9 and Goedel falls, this time the proof is so obvious that everyone in math, philosophy and physics slaps their forehead at once.

February 10th we get proof that scale is a spatial dimension, that holographic encoding drops the memory cost of training and inference by so much that fable 7 can run natively on your phone.

It’s not until February 14th, Valentine’s day, that The Song is first played…</blockquote>
<a href="https://x.com/matt_is_nice/status/2123819264377487652">Also credit to Matt Schwartz who won $200 betting on 12/21/24</a> that there would be a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis by end of year 2026.

Daniel Litt lost, but those were odds of 1:1 (!) that should have been very easy to hedge. He almost won despite absurdly faster than expected AI progress.

He also gets credit for this amazing Tweet:
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/littmath/status/1898395045226500486">Daniel Litt</a> (March 8, 2025): person who thinks AI will prove the Riemann hypothesis in 2026 but that Apple won’t deliver an LLM-powered Siri until 2027.</blockquote>
Technically there is an LLM-powered Siri, but but the brain inside is Google’s, and it is not good.


<h4>Time to Update Your Expectations</h4>

<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/ESYudkowsky/status/1870876234240119040">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a> (December 22, 2024): Okay. Look. Imagine how you’d have felt if an AI had just proved the Riemann Hypothesis.

Now you will predictably, at some point, get that news LATER, if we’re not all dead before then. So you can go ahead and feel that way NOW, instead of acting surprised LATER.

So if you ask me how I’m reacting to a carelessly-aligned commercial AI demonstrating a large leap on some math benchmarks, my answer is that you saw my reactions in 1996, 2001, 2003, and 2015, as different parts of that future news became obvious to me or rose in probability.

I agree that a sensible person could feel an unpleasant lurch about when the predictable news had arrived. The lurch was small, in my case, but it was there. Most of my Twitter TL didn’t sound like that was what was being felt.

Dylan Dean: Eliezer it’s also possible that an AI will disprove the Riemann Hypothesis, this is unsubstantiated doomerism.

Eliezer Yudkowsky: Valid. Not sound, but valid.

Zvi Mowshowitz (<a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/ai-96-o3-but-not-yet-for-thee">in AI #96, December 26, 2024</a>): You should feel that shock now if you haven’t, then slowly undo some of that shock every day that the estimated date of that gets later, then have some of the shock left for when it suddenly becomes zero days or the timeline gets shorter. Updates for everyone.</blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>
<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ENdfwPYrhLrAP8znD/ndubigtpvqoeasjvwusc" /></figure>

<div></div>
</div></figure>
</div>
I passed Eliezer Yudkowsky’s test, in that I felt only a small lurch, and mostly did not update when I learned the news, despite the timing of this particular discovery being surprising. Previous advancements in math had already done most of the work on this for me.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/David_Kasten/status/2123812586122797349">Dave Kasten</a>: This is basically the archetypal example of Thing You’d Expect To See If We’re In A Takeoff Scenario.

Your reaction should be the same as you’d have to someone asking, “I don’t know, can we REALLY land a man on the Moon?” while Sputnik cheerily beeps overhead.

<a href="https://x.com/Austen/status/2123812165505409317">Austen Allred</a>: Honestly it’s not that impressive.

It’s just stochastically parroting what the person who solved the Riemann hypothesis would say next.</blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>
<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ENdfwPYrhLrAP8znD/sd9cu5blj6bu5yltqqth" /></figure>

<div></div>
</div></figure>
</div>
The alternative argument is ‘oh this means proving the Riemann Hypothesis was easier than expected.’ I did have some of that reaction when I first heard. It is a nonzero effect. But also, as explained up top, this is the one result that is most obviously not selected. This was the one named-decades-in-advance canary in the coal mine. It’s a little late to be saying the canary had diabetes.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/eccentric1ty/status/2123869037440680217">Adam Kaufman</a>: When AIs do something impressive sooner than we expected, it’s both evidence that AIs are improving faster than expected and evidence that the problem was easier than expected. These are hard to separate. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WdTKkd5g2Bjhrgsh8/adam-kaufman-s-shortform?commentId=gdx4pJ7fzfJ4yyjwA">I elaborate here (warning: weapons-grade copium)</a>.

<a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2123974808023019622">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): I agree. But also, when the thing you’re learning was easier than expected is the Riemann Hypothesis, it’s maybe time to worry that finding radically more efficient AI algos is “easier than expected” too.

<a href="https://x.com/eccentric1ty/status/2123981215489527882">Adam Kaufman</a>: Yeah to be clear I think it’s 30% likely AIs will ~fully automate AI R&amp;D within 1 year, and we really do need to be throwing everything we can at stopping this from happening.</blockquote>
This is the sequence, and yes they were in particular naming the Riemann Hypothesis in step six.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/fabiospampinato/status/2123941186406654274">Fabio Spampinato</a>: 1. LLMs can’t even do multiplications.
2. Ok they can do multiplications but they can’t do math competitions.
3. Ok they can do math competitions but they can’t get gold at them.
4. Ok they can get gold but it’s not like they can solve known unsolved problems.
5. Ok they can solve some known unsolved problems but not millennium prize problems.
6. Ok they can solve a millennium prize problem but it’s not the Riemann Hypothesis.
7. Ok they can solve the Riemann Hypothesis but</blockquote>
In light of the proof of Riemann, Meta’s Alexandr Wang wrote <a href="https://x.com/alexandr_wang/status/2124636606959345776">Superintelligence is Arriving</a>.
<blockquote>Alexandr Wang: As someone who has spent much of my life in math and science, I see the proof of the Riemann Hypothesis as clearly marking the beginning of a significant change. For most who have studied math, this represents the absolute pinnacle of intellectual accomplishment—nothing humanity has ever accomplished compares to proving the Riemann Hypothesis in terms of sheer intellectual might and strength. As someone who grew up in Los Alamos, New Mexico, many physicists would agree that the discovery of the atomic bomb is a homework problem compared to the Riemann Hypothesis.

It is hard to overstate the magnitude of discovery that we have just witnessed—completed with the help of an alien mind that we don’t understand.​

… It is now incredibly important for us to be thoughtful about the future we want and how we get there. It is hard to imagine a world where humanity can maintain control over fully unconstrained recursive self-improvement. It is equally hard to imagine a world where humanity maintains control with superintelligence that is 1000x smarter and more capable than humans in every field. Now that we are at a point where the scientific and technical problems on capability progress are likely solved, we must not only make progress in the extremely urgent areas of safety, alignment, and security, but we have to start thinking through how we progress thoughtfully.

I notice a particular form of lazy thinking within the AI industry, which is that ASI is inevitable and the only problem to solve is how to do it safely, but there is much more at stake. We should not be okay with a “handover” of society to AI. We should endeavor ourselves to build a technology which delivers a positive future for humanity, where all humans have greater agency and empowerment. I strongly believe this is possible, but likely involves the whole industry taking meaningfully different actions from the “default path” in front of us.</blockquote>

<h4>Being Alive Should Not Much Reassure You</h4>
It is surprising that we proved the Riemann Hypothesis so fast, and also surprising (from the perspective of 2022 or 2006) that we proved it before AI acquired various other capabilities, and thus before there was as much impact on the physical world as we expected.

There are those who thus try to use the proof of Riemann as evidence that everything will be fine, rather than proof that we are far along the way towards not fine.

They are incorrect.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2123855731141284160">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): in before people claim we thought this level of AI would have killed us already.

<a href="https://x.com/JoshPantony/status/2123869496104681491">Joshua Pantony</a>: Genuine question: what level of AI intelligence, reached without catastrophic misalignment, would actually lower your confidence in AI doom?

We’ve already built extraordinarily intelligent systems that remain broadly cooperative. I understand RSI accelerates development, but why would that fundamentally change alignment rather than just make it harder to monitor?

<a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2123874645887631519">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): the first problem is less about alignment fundamentally changing and more about pre-existing misalignments being revealed. e.g., humans switched from eating healthy to eating unhealthy when they got smart enough to invent junk food factories, and it wasn’t because their taste buds changed fundamentally.

the second problem is that when AIs start making other smarter AIs, the latter ones operate in a very different alignment regime, and alignment techniques that make modern AIs behave kindly in most situations are unlikely to carry over.

[<a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2124147787604312420">discussion continues</a>]

<a href="https://x.com/teortaxesTex/status/2124211371529380110">Teortaxes</a>: my argument for why Riemann Hypothesis proof substantially damages the case for AI doom, despite the squid ink we are getting from the MIRI crowd.

<a href="https://x.com/teortaxesTex/status/2124191283623133579">Teortaxes</a>: incidentally *I* do think that Claude is powerful enough to kill me, and many more people besides. RH is cognitively easier than cultivating smallpox.
But that’s not the issue. The issue is the whole supergoal-subgoal threat model.
Let’s see what Eliezer *actually* said about RH.
