<div></div>
</div></figure>
</div>
<div>
<figure>
<div>
<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ENdfwPYrhLrAP8znD/uyelgvjgvojcr4fqblnd" /></figure>

<div></div>
</div></figure>
</div>
<a href="https://x.com/teortaxesTex/status/2124207815401083063">Teortaxes</a>: “But it was just an example, you simpleton!” – is the obvious comeback. I don’t think this is good enough, Eliezer.
Trivially, the onus is on you to choose your examples well. [continues]

<a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2124236057034285448">roon</a> (OpenAI): these are all good points, but it’s also basically true that strategic and situational thinking in language models has far lagged their skill in other ways. it does seem that the way in episode reward seeking is set up, it really wouldn’t make sense to attempt what Sable does here.

<a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2124407658979725685">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: &gt; That is why you and so many others had the intuition that it’d require a qualitatively superior, larger, uncontrollable mind to do this. That is why math is hailed as grand magick and the language of God.

You’re confused. I have never been unclear on the distinction between “RLVR works on it” and the neighboring “Can be derived from simple premises with near-certainty” and the faraway “Requires crazy amounts of compute to derive simply, actually takes realtime interaction with an open-world impossibly complicated environment you can only model partially and probabilistically.”

Trying to leap on the choice of “Solve the Riemann hypothesis” as an example of a problem on which somebody would try to dump a lot of AI compute, in a case where we need to just pick a damn simple example for the undergrad-equivalent readers — you are just reaching far, far too hard for a desperate attempt to preserve your original smear attempt.

<a href="https://x.com/Douthat60/status/2124260136453800150">Ross Douthat</a> (QTing Teortaxes): Interesting argument about the Riemann result and updating theories of AI doom.

<a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2124406223101665763">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: This is like a physicist writing about a rolling cylinder in a vacuum to explain some elementary concept with the simplest possible terms, and somebody going, “You think if you make a vacuum, it materializes a rolling cylinder!”

This is like a physicist writing about a rolling cylinder in a vacuum to explain some elementary concept with the simplest possible terms, and somebody going, “You think if you make a vacuum, it materializes a rolling cylinder!”

Ask any frontier LLM, or any other entity that can pass the LSAT. <a href="https://x.com/grok">@Grok</a>, can you ELI5 why inventing this thought experiment doesn’t imply the author believes, “If an AI can prove the Riemann hypothesis, the world must end”?

<a href="https://x.com/grok/status/2124406593252892743">Grok</a>: Imagine a physics teacher talks about a rolling cylinder in empty space to explain friction simply. That doesn’t mean the teacher thinks vacuum creates cylinders. The example just makes the idea clear and easy.

Same here. Using “AI proves Riemann” is a simple stand-in to talk about very capable AI and the risks that can come with it. It doesn’t mean the author thinks solving that one math problem equals the world ending. It’s just a clean illustration, not a prediction or a claim about that specific task.</blockquote>
The argument from Teortaxes here is quite poor, both the quoted part and the remainder. It argues that because the book’s fictional scenario used the Riemann Hypothesis as its example of a hard problem, and the problem turned out easier than that, the entire argument about doom is invalid or discredited.

That seems deeply stupid to me. The part of the scenario in <a href="https://amzn.to/4iwvCtW"><em>If Anyone Builds It, Everyone Dies</em></a> that mattered was ‘Sable was given an extremely hard set of problems that would require massively more compute and capability to solve.’ The details of those problems are irrelevant. The choice of Riemann as an example of one of the problems was because it is so recognizable, and being able to more easily solve one of the many problems given does not change the logic of the scenario within the book even a tiny bit.

The Riemann Hypothesis is uniquely useful as a universal indication of the growth of AI capabilities in mathematics. It is not specifically important in the context of the problems being assigned to Sable in <a href="https://amzn.to/4iwvCtW"><em>If Anyone Builds It, Everyone Dies</em></a><em>. </em>Using it as the canonical example within a group of such problems in a fictional scenario does not constitute a prediction that solving this problem in particular required a takeover. Nor, if that prediction had been made, would that be load bearing on the other claims made in the book.

So yes, <a href="https://x.com/Douthat60/status/2123970537814683742">we have dodged the literal Riemann Hypothesis Catastrophe</a>, where an AI asked to solve the Riemann Hypothesis converts everything into computronium. That particular example of a hard problem will not come to pass. It does not prevent the same logical sequence from happening regarding a different hard task or problem.

It is more like this, assuming you extend the timeline out well past the point where the exponential growth of the resulting process would run up against the humans:
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/JeffLadish/status/2124747373721301489">Jeffrey Ladish</a> (Palisade Research): AI proved the Riemann hypothesis and hasn’t killed us all. Does that mean we’re fine? Of course not. But if AI can build robots that build more robots, and datacenters that build more datacenters, and humans aren’t all dead or subjugated, THEN we’re probably fine.

AI proving the Riemann hypothesis is a fair bit of evidence that robots able to build more robots are probably not far away. Math / the digital world and the physical world are not somehow separate, non-overlapping magisteria

<a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2124762882965688794">j⧉nus</a>: agreed.</blockquote>
<a href="https://x.com/EpistemicHope/status/2124703087268860291">What update is appropriate in the face</a> of ‘Riemann was solved and we are not dead?’ The biggest thing to me is that we learned about the nature of the jaggedness of AI capability gains. The lack of other physical world impacts so far makes us not being dead unsurprising.

However, if the labs are correct and we get recursive self-improvement (RSI) within the year, as a direct result of the same type of capabilities that enabled this proof, then we may quickly after that stop being not dead, and our failure to be dead would start to be substantially stronger evidence.


<h4>Too Many Words</h4>
Why is the proof 250,000 pages long?
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/__alpoge__/status/2124987173439209561">levent</a>: Stupid things I did:
1. 250,000+ pages was a joke guys!, I find big number extremely hilarious. I tried to make it clear by the level of pedantry of the reals total ordered statement in the main theorem, but people ran with it unfortunately and I didn’t feel like talking on social media in the days after the announcement, though I did have a good time with the memes. Sorry!!! (Similarly with our YM4 existence I found it extremely funny that we were slowly working through 60,000 pages of slop, though the committee asking for Lean made me feel dumb about wasting our time with that. But 60,000 funny.) Tao advises to not joke in papers, see also the Big Lebowski reference I shoehorned, I now understand why… I didn’t feel comfortable having any slop writeup attached to this problem, and I figured I can’t produce anything readable anyway, so I went off the Lean to lengthmaxx.
2. The Lean is full of garbage obviously, but compiles, but all criticism of such garbage is totally reasonable and accepted, I should have cleaned it up but it was randomly difficult to for various strange reasons. Mea culpa…
3. @scottnarmstrong clocked me, indeed even as a student there were theorems that took me the better part of a decade to write up, but with this one I should hopefully be more responsible. (I really was yoloing a lot in there, I bet most is unnecessary in the end, admittedly the lazy thing to do would be to wait because it’ll simplify massively…)
4. I forgot to emphasize what I think is the point. Arguments like these, cool, but they are the raw inputs when interpolating definitions. We don’t have the / an “H^1 object” over number fields, and probably for a layperson the fact that we fight hard for these arguments for the purpose of definitions is not clear a priori (that’s at least my philosophy). Just like I knew I must fight for an utterly simple Weil / Deligne, I feel Riemann should be easy, and any real difficulty is an indication our setup is just dumb at the moment.

<a href="https://x.com/scottnarmstrong/status/2125130321930682731">Scott Armstrong</a>: There are certain annoying/funny things about working with the agents to do math and one is that they write things in very pedantic ways. So for instance they may inflate the size of a proof written in text by a factor of 10 or more by writing all kinds of irrelevant details. They also write things in very stupid ways that humans don’t like. We call this kind of output “slop”. This doesn’t mean what the agents write is wrong, just incredibly hard to read, sometimes it’s almost funny.

If you first write the proof in the formal language Lean (in some sense the most pedantic way possible) and THEN ask the agents to *translate* that proof into natural language, you can often get another 10x on the length, resulting an insanely and hideously long, almost hilariously long text.

Levent did this and giggled to himself about the &gt; 250 000 pages it generated, because this is objectively hilarious. I agree with him that this is funny, and if you don’t think this is funny I don’t know what to tell you.

Best estimates of the “real” length of his proof are in the range 1000-2000 pages, before simplification. So many people quoting the 250k+ pages are very confused by this.</blockquote>
This is still not as many pages as the planning documentation for the proposed tunnel under the Thames connecting Kent and Essex, <a href="https://x.com/Ale_xRich/status/2124520032340214227">which is already 360,000 pages and cost 297 million pounds</a>. For less than half that, Norway built the world’s longest road tunnel.


<h4>How Much Did Levent Matter Here?</h4>
There is little question that Levent is got Claude to solve the Riemann Hypothesis, and other big problems, earlier than we would have otherwise solved those problems. The question is, how much credit should he get for that? How did he do it? How much of it involved having access to better internal models and throwing a lot of compute at the problems, versus making his own contributions?
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/mvernal/status/2123929725160816971">Mike Vernal</a>: I do not know @__alpoge__ (though I assume he is well-known in math circles). But I feel like a detail being under-appreciated in the “Claude solved X, Claude solved Y” narrative is that this is an insane CV (and so I will go out on a limb and assume he would be a Fields Medalist-caliber mathematician with or without Claude).

Is this not a reason to just be optimistic? That brilliant people with incredible tools will just pull forward progress decades if not centuries?
