<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/SebastienBubeck/status/2125402576145572334">Sébastien Bubeck</a> (OpenAI): As part of the October 6 release we had a particularly clean advance on the matrix multiplication exponent, with a new upper bound of 9/4. Since then, the model has been working on a LOWER BOUND for this exponent. This is already pretty crazy since NOTHING was known for lower bounds! Today we announced that actually 9/4 is tight … This is really the kind of result that I never thought I would see in my lifetime.

(btw, by an “old” result of Yin Tat Lee and collaborators this also implies that linear programs can be solved in time n^{9/4}.)

<a href="https://x.com/deedydas/status/2125445769629204816">Deedy</a>: OpenAI just dropped a 111-page proof of a 57-year old math problem: the matrix multiplication exponent (ω) is exactly 9/4.

This problem ranks #31 on ProofAtlas’ top 500 open problems in math and is arguably 2nd only to P = NP in algorithms.

The problem: How many steps does it take to multiply two n×n matrices? The schoolbook method takes n^3. The true cost is n^ω. What is ω?

The history:
– 1969: Strassen beats 3 for the first time: 2.807
– 1981: Schönhage, 2.522
– 1990: Coppersmith-Winograd, 2.376
– 1990 to Aug 2026: 36 years to shave off 0.005, 2.3712
– Nov 2026: OpenAI proves the upper and lower bound is 2.25

Wild to see things we were taught in university be changed right before our very eyes.</blockquote>

<h4>You Deserve a Medal</h4>
The math world is slow to verify. Right now Kalshi only has Riemann ‘resolved’ before 2027 at 74%, and Polymarket on November 20 only gave 26% that Clay declared it solved. What happens down the line? Who gets the Fields Medal?
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2123871074391822470">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): A lot of people are wondering whether Claude will be awarded a fields medal. But the next fields medals aren’t awarded until July 2030, so. At this point it’s not entirely clear it’ll get the opportunity.</blockquote>
How should humans compete with each other on math going forward?
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/kevinroose/status/2123819673653551323">Kevin Roose</a>: I am extremely not a mathematician so this could be a bad take, but it seems like they probably have to stop giving out Fields medals and other big math prizes soon, right?

If AI is solving all of the hardest open problems, why give awards to the people at the labs who have unfair advantages via free tokens and unreleased model access? It would be like letting the programmers of Stockfish win every chess tournament.

One solution of course would be to do what they’ve done in chess, and make AI-assisted proofs ineligible for awards. But then you’re not really recognizing top achievements anymore. (Also, probably impossible to police mathematicians using AI.)

Oh well, I guess I can rest easy knowing this existential crisis is only coming to math, and not anything I personally do or care about!</blockquote>
There are at least two paths forward on this. One option is to award prizes only to fully understood proofs. You can use AI for help, since we can’t prove that you didn’t, but you need to be able to defend the final product. Math, as the mathematicians keep saying, should be about understanding.

The other option, which I prefer, is to say that the AI proofs count. Yes, that means the awards will, at least for a time, go to those with access to internal models at the top labs. That is not ‘fair.’ I am okay with that. Life is not fair, in this sense. Most people lack the talent or opportunity to do math for real either way.


<h4>Math as Understanding</h4>
<a href="https://x.com/ThomasVanRiet2/status/2124449626098892892">Here is one Fields Medal winner reacting hopefully to the news</a>.
<blockquote>Chau Ngo<strong>:</strong> Riemann formulated his famous hypothesis in his only paper in number theory “on the number of primes less than a given magnitude”. In this paper, Riemann discovered an extraordinary duality between prime numbers and zeros of the zeta function. He observed that if all nontrivial zeros have real part 1/2, the Riemann hypothesis (RH), then the number of primes less than a given magnitude must satisfy a very strong asymptotic law. A weaker version of this asymptotic law, known as the prime number theorem, was later derived from a weaker version of RH i.e there is a zero free region slightly larger than the obvious one Re(s)&gt;1.

The reaction to the Anthropic Alpoge event displays a profound misconception about mathematics and the work of mathematicians. We are still so attached to the traditional benchmark which put high values on proofs of hard conjectures, RH is believed to be one of the hardest. This measure was pretty accurate because to be able to prove a hard conjecture, mathematician usually need to have a deep understanding of a whole area of mathematics around it. The proof is valuable not only because it settles a long standing problem but because it would further and deepen our understanding of a part of mathematics.

It is obvious that AI generated proofs do not offer by themselves broader or deeper understanding of mathematics, or any new insight, unless we study it real hard. But that makes it quite futile our usual competition in proving hard conjectures, because the machine is now doing it much better than us. But there is no point to feel sad for being less capable than a machine in performing certain tasks. We must consider AI output as a part of nature, or a source of data, for us to scrutinize and theorize.

Some people toll the bell for mathematical research a little bit too fast. I believe in contrary that it will further flourish because it will democratize. It used to be the case that to perform at the top, one needs to be born with certain special predispositions to feel and arrange complex proofs. That part may now be dealt with by AI. What will make the difference may be the ability to ask good questions, to have a sense of observation and a deep desire to deepen understanding.

If Riemann lives in the current days, nothing would prevents him from developing to completion his duality between prime numbers and zeros of the zeta function. It still requires Riemann’s genius to come up with this duality that nobody has conjectured before. But AI may have helped him to prove his hypothesis.</blockquote>
Humans are still, so far, better at asking the questions. For now, that is the thing to do.


<h4>Timing Is Everything</h4>
The IPO forced Levent to wait a few days to announce the result. Rather than an attempt to line up the result with the IPO, this was a delay to avoid impacting the IPO.
<blockquote><a href="https://alpo.ge/remark.pdf">Levent Alpöge</a>: ​The precise timing is horrible.

… This all happened for me late Sunday night, and I messaged Dario, “Hypothetically, if I were to have . . . “, eventually asking about my ability to announce. We went and figured it out and the fact is that there was a constraint to respect, though I did ask about progressively more bizarre ideas.

… Our leadership and the few trusted mathematicians I consulted hold extremely similar views on the right things to do in these circumstances (it is occasionally possible for physicists and mathematicians to agree. . . ), and I am clearly unilaterally responsible for feeling I just could not live if I waited even longer. There is an obvious cynical view, to do with the timing, but it is false, and I am pleased that I am in circumstances where it is obviously false.</blockquote>
Not that this changed anything, because as the next section says, the market does not care.


<h4>The Market Does Not Care</h4>
You know what is cool? As we learned this week, a trillion dollars.

You know what else is cool? In my opinion, proving the Riemann Hypothesis.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/nabeelqu/status/2123806732027924920">Nabeel S. Qureshi</a>: Claude solved the Riemann hypothesis and
Anthropic stock is down 10%. Seems the market is not “RSI-pilled” and believes in ‘AI as normal technology’.</blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>
<div>
<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ENdfwPYrhLrAP8znD/ozr1urwj05dniz1omr8y" /></figure>

<div></div>
</div>
</div></figure>
</div>
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2123946595955953844">roon</a> (OpenAI): ANTHROPIC ANNOUNCES FAST TAKEOFF AS PROBES EXIT THE HELIOPAUSE ($ANT-7%)</blockquote>
The market disagrees. The market does not care. It does not think this is cool, or valuable, or important. Any effect of proving Riemann was massively overwhelmed by the loss of the IPO bounce, the absence of Jensen Huang from Nvidia and a general tech market decline. On Monday Anthropic again underperformed.

The market has only somewhat taken even the first AI pill. To say that the market thinks of AI as a ‘normal technology’ is if anything to be generous to the market. The market is pricing in the value of superintelligence, and the things AI is almost certainly going to be able to do even a year from now, at virtually zero.

This is part of a long pattern. Developments in AI will point to short timelines and faster capability advances. The market will not care. Then people will cite the market not caring as evidence that the event did not matter.

If you say ‘this was priced in,’ I say no. It was very obviously not priced in.

I am done pretending that the market is being anything other than an idiot about this.

Does that mean that the AI stocks are cheap? Nothing I say is ever investment advice, and you cannot benefit from your Anthropic stock if you are dead or sufficiently disempowered. There are many other reasons to not be so confident about the net present value of future cash flows, or to think number might go down for a while.

What I do know is that the most important value of Anthropic is not priced in.

<a href="https://x.com/andywalters/status/2124610756968579271">Perhaps the market would have cared more if we had proved the Ream Man Hypothesis</a> (2 minute video, mandatory).<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ENdfwPYrhLrAP8znD/claude-and-levent-alpoege-prove-the-riemann-hypothesis#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [A Therapist's Guide to the Singularity](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2nuNydxTyMPneLRdH/a-therapist-s-guide-to-the-singularity)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm a therapist who has been following AI alignment for the last few years. In that time, I've been dumbfounded by two things. First, how fast capabilities have progressed throughout this year. We expected things to move fast and they still managed to exceed our expectations. Second, how few people grasp the gravity of the situation, particularly within my own field.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mental health clinicians are tasked with helping people navigate society's blind spots, so we need to understand what is coming. Yet it is rare to find another clinician who truly understands the real implications of AI. Most of them dismiss it as a hype bubble, or as an exaggeration the labs use to get regulatory capture, largely because they don't see what makes AI different from past technological shifts.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I want this post to be a first resource for the stress and anxiety currently plaguing AI researchers, with the hope of developing more resources in the future. I'm also working on training licensed therapists to bring them up to speed, and I'll have a question for you about that at the end.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Above all, remember that you are not alone. Many people on this forum and around the world share your concerns, even if they rarely talk about it openly. People from all walks of life are working really hard to make sure alignment gets solved and this transition goes well.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What's happening to AI researchers</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From the studies I've read</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-exR857gJAAamWnZet-1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-exR857gJAAamWnZet-1">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and the AI researchers I've talked to, many in the field are operating on high alert. That state can be useful in short bursts, but the problem is that researchers have been stuck in this hypervigilant state for years. They check the news and Twitter anxiously. They use guilt and anxiety as fuel to keep pushing harder. A lot of researchers work in brutally fast sprints that deplete their energy and result in burnout. They feel ashamed of their own human limitations, and that shame drives them right back to another sprint.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What makes this even worse is that researchers can't exactly tell the organization they work for about the stress. If they express how much strain they are under, they might not seem like a team player. If they tell their friends and family, that might add stress to their lives. So researchers suppress their concerns. They hide their fear of a human catastrophe or loss of control and their grief for a world that will be lost soon.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Right now, there is almost no infrastructure to help them navigate this. Having found only one support group for AI researchers, I decided to make this guide.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name it</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suppression is a very bad idea. Right now, if you tell somebody what is going on, they will instinctively try to downplay the risk. Part of your mind sees RSI starting to kick in, along with models that can already handle a big chunk of remote work. Another part sees that everyone around you is fine and life seems normal. So the first step is to name what you are experiencing. You can't convince everyone and that is not the goal. The goal here is to put your thoughts into words, because naming them gives you much more control over them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Start by naming specifically what you see. Why is AI different from other technologies? What is it like to know your friends and family are unaware of what is about to happen? How fast is the world changing? Then name the fears that keep you up at night. The world is definitely going to change. Maybe that means mass job loss. Maybe it means we all die. If we survive, could we have permanent authoritarian rule? Maybe it means our relationship to the world is forever altered in a way it has never been before. Name your worry that your kids won't have a future. Name the responsibility you feel for the world you are helping create. You might feel grandiose when saying it out loud. There is no judgment here. Take a pen and paper and write out exactly what you are seeing, what you are thinking and what specifically you fear. Name how unfair and cruel this situation is, and really dig deep into that.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then ask how you wanted your life to go. Don't glance over this question. Sit down and think. What were you expecting when you were in college? Did you want to have kids? What were you thinking about your parents? What kind of work did you want to contribute to the world? Think about what you wanted, then think about where we are now and grieve the gap between them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even the "good" outcome is a loss, and this is very important to name. Just because we don't end up in a catastrophic scenario does not mean everything is fine. The old world ends either way. Even if we get abundance, most of the Americans I've worked with have a great deal of their identity in their jobs. They get meaning from being members of a community they contribute to. What happens when that identity is radically transformed? What is going to fill that vacuum? This transition will be very stressful no matter how it goes.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Keeping a clear head</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The fear of a loss of control event or a permanent dictatorship is a valid concern, and it's important to remember that. The real challenge is ensuring a valid fear doesn't turn into irrational thinking. There are two tools we can use to prevent that.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The first is the one LessWrong loves, which is rationality. Much like in cognitive behavioral therapy, we can audit our own thoughts to check if they are rational. Are we using black and white thinking? Are we using logical fallacies? Are we minimizing? Is there evidence for these claims?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The second is sitting with the thing we fear most. Irrationality thrives on fear. What we fear is a version of reality that we find unacceptable. If there is something we can't face, we will distort reality to keep it away. So ask yourself: what exactly is unacceptable to you? Is it all of us getting wiped out? Is it that this is not crazy and it is all really happening? What if humans aren't in charge of which direction we are heading? Can you face it and keep working, despite how terrifying the future might be? The more we sit with it, the more we move toward acceptance and the more clearly we can see the truth.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Taking your mind back</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you're working 80 hours a week, I'm not going to tell you to drop to 40. That isn't realistic, and it isn't my place to decide how you spend your life. What I can recommend is to try small steps instead. If this is on your mind all day, can you take back 10 minutes this week? Even 5? Going from 80 hours to 79 isn't really about the time difference. It's about teaching ourselves that we have control over our minds. When you've been on high alert for a long time, it can feel like your mind is a motor that can't turn off. We want to be careful not to stay stuck at this level of alertness, because eventually we are going to crash. Choosing 5 minutes where you don't have to think about any of this is a way to reclaim your sense of agency. Right now the fear is in control, and every small, intentional pause reminds you that you are still in control. If you love working 80 hours a week and it feels like your choice, that's okay too. The question is if you're the one choosing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A lot of people compare this moment to a wartime effort and argue that we need to push as hard as we can. Yet even during wartime, there were breaks. Soldiers were rotated out for rest, because recovery is also a form of strategy. An easy way to reflect on this is to think about your coworker: Would you be okay with your coworker working 80 hours a week? If not, why? There's a point of diminishing returns, and they wouldn't be functioning well. So when you're in a really stressful stretch, think of yourself as your own coworker, or if you're a boss, ask how you would treat someone else. Sometimes you need to step outside the role you're in to see clearly.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I want to be concrete here, because the usual mental health advice is basically "leave it at work and live normally," which is not very considerate in the situation we find ourselves in. So here is the question I'll ask you: </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What do you want to do before the old world is gone?</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Start small. Once a day, have one thing you can look forward to, even for a few minutes, like watching a sunset, playing video games or calling a friend. Once a week, maybe it's dinner with the people you care about or yoga. Once a month, maybe it's a trip to the redwoods.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then think bigger and write a bucket list. Do you want to make dinner for your mom? Hike Mount Rainier? Tell your parents how much you love them? Reconnect with an old friend you miss? Go stargazing or see a concert? Maybe you really hurt someone in the past, it's been on your mind and now you don't know how much time is left. Reaching out to them is a possibility. Once you commit to something, put it in your calendar.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">During these breaks, enforce a strict "no AI talk" rule. Put your phone in a different room. If you get dinner with friends who are also researchers, make a rule not to talk about any AI stuff. If we do end up in a terrifying loss of control scenario a few years from now, you'll be glad you made time for these moments while you could.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Finding a therapist</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is too much stress for anyone to handle and you need support. I would recommend finding a professional to talk to. Your therapist doesn't need to fully understand AI to help you. A lot of us help people grieve a parent when we haven't lost our own. We are trained explicitly on how to navigate situations we have not experienced ourselves. The tools stay the same for rumination, loss of agency, anxiety and grief. There are very few AGI pilled therapists that I'm aware of, and others have described them as "scarce and expensive."</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-exR857gJAAamWnZet-2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-exR857gJAAamWnZet-2">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you're comfortable, spend the first session or two teaching them, slowly, maybe even with a slide deck. What is RSI? What is alignment? Why is intelligence different from every other capability we've ever gained? Why can nobody stop? What is your role? I know this is painful and exhausting when you're already exhausted. This context can give them a deeper understanding when working with you.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Next, say your fears out loud before they do. As you have probably experienced, people are likely to think you're catastrophizing, and your therapist might too. Tell them up front: "I think you'll think I'm catastrophizing. You'll say I'm irrational or that I have a grandiose complex. You'll tell me to just work less." Be honest about what you are thinking and what you are concerned about, and explain why this is actually different. This can really speed up the process of building rapport. Trust is a two way street. A therapeutic relationship also takes time to bloom, and sometimes a therapist just won't be a good fit. That's completely fine. Keep trying different therapists. I'm sure you'll eventually find someone willing to hold space for you through all this. Please don't give up hope.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On confidentiality and NDAs, I hope people understand that we know how to be discreet, and there are laws that keep us quiet. A therapist legally cannot go around telling people what you said, and the licensing board exists to protect you. This is why I highly advise against a life coach. If you have an NDA, tell us up front that there's material you can't go into, then give us the general shape of the thing and leave out the specifics. We know how to navigate tricky situations. The main exceptions are if you are a danger to yourself or others or if there is a court order.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Just as a note, if you pay cash, there's no insurance company that can require session notes to be sent over. If you also ask for handwritten notes and say you're uncomfortable with a lot of detail being written down, nothing detailed goes into any system. In this scenario, on the very unlikely chance anyone tried to get hold of your notes, how would they even know if or which therapist you went to? Nobody can subpoena what they don't know exists.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The isolation</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Try not to carry this alone. Would it be helpful to tell your friends and family? Why or why not? What do you hope to gain by doing so? Does sharing with them feel scary? A lot of people want to protect the ones they love from this, which is completely reasonable. If you do want to share, you don't have to go into deep technical detail. You can simply be honest about what you are experiencing and say that you believe the world is going to look very different soon. If opening up to family feels like too much, is there a coworker or someone who already knows as much as you do that you could start with? Who you tell, and why you're holding back, is something you can figure out on your own or with a therapist.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A lot of people won't understand what you're carrying until it's too late, or they might never understand it at all, because alignment gets solved, the danger never comes and everyone assumes your anxiety was just acting up. That's a deeply lonely position to hold. It's worth sitting with what that means and coming to terms with the isolation it brings.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the meantime, consider starting an informal support group with a few other researchers, even one day a week, where you say your fears and concerns out loud to each other. If there is one lesson we can take from past crises, it is that connection brings strength.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do you want an AGI pilled therapist?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm thinking about training licensed therapists in the Bay area, London and maybe New York, remotely. Then I'd build a directory of those therapists, so researchers could find a clinician who has a working understanding of basic concepts like RSI, collective action problems and model welfare. The training would be free to clinicians. I'm still figuring out how to execute on this, and I first wanted to gauge if a directory like that is something you would use.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thank you for reading. Please let me know if you have any questions.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-exR857gJAAamWnZet-1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-exR857gJAAamWnZet-1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/ews9xbzjGGfR64oum/mental-health-challenges-in-the-ai-safety-ecosystem-seem"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mental health challenges in the AI safety ecosystem</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-exR857gJAAamWnZet-2"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-exR857gJAAamWnZet-2">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TuHhSxGYgSudKeDtb/psychological-support-for-ai-safety-researchers-is-neglected"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Psychological Support for AI Safety Researchers Is Neglected and Easy to Provide</span></a></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2nuNydxTyMPneLRdH/a-therapist-s-guide-to-the-singularity#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### ["A Republic of LessWrong Letters"—A Letter to DirectedEvolution](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RjydrDjY8sgWy2rav/a-republic-of-lesswrong-letters-a-letter-to)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My dearest </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/directedevolution?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@DirectedEvolution</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We've </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9EJJrKiYEAmaP2TcL/rats-just-wanna-have-fun" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">both</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/kmruNMEMK5uN3LH6n/notes-on-the-decline-of-lesswrong-s-great-writers" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">been</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9e2nh92HurD7bWJMR/no-comment" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">thinking</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/p9R8JA6hygSLyMoTg/contra-pace-on-the-decline-of-great-writing-on-lesswrong" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">about</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> how to strengthen the writing culture on LessWrong. I think we agree that LessWrong has many great writers (more, I'd say) when compared to the past, and yet still feel that </span><i><em>something</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> has been lost.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You raise an excellent hypothesis in your recent essay </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9e2nh92HurD7bWJMR/no-comment" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No Comment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It's not that the writers are less intellectually sophisticated, or their ideas less good, or their writing less fun (though they should be </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9EJJrKiYEAmaP2TcL/rats-just-wanna-have-fun" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">having more fun!</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). The primary change is that we're talking with </span><i><em>each other</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> less than we used to.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You point out that LessWrong authors comment less than they used to, and while I'd add that the absolute amount of writing is up, we can be absolutely sure of your claim on a relative level. Recall this chart from Lightcone's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5n2ZQcbc7r4R8mvqc/the-lightcone-is-nothing-without-its-people" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2024 fundraiser</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795547233/lexical_client_uploads/gmslm9kg4gf9ic3hudmb.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(I have updated it to include the last two years of data.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Again, absolute wordcount is up. However, after we realize that the light blue corresponds to posts and the dark blue to comments, we can see that LessWrong 1.0 was characterized by a roughly 10-to-1 ratio of comments to posts, and LessWrong 2.0 is about a 1-to-1 ratio of comments to posts. This is a </span><i><em>drastic</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> change in site culture.</span></p><p><b><strong>Historical aside.</strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> You probably know this, but in case not (and also because I like getting the chance to talk about it), the change is largely due to the algorithm for showing posts on the frontpage.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On LessWrong 2.0, we have an algorithm for showing posts that is directly proportional to recency and karma. On LessWrong 1.0 </span><i><em>there was no algorithm</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, it just showed whatever the latest post was that had been posted. Even if it was utter trash, everyone on LessWrong was looking at it together, the eye of Sauron turning its gaze to you, and this was the topic that everyone was speaking about. The comments were intense and multitudinous.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now that there is any sane sorting function at all, there is no longer a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9QxnfMYccz9QRgZ5z/the-costly-coordination-mechanism-of-common-knowledge" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">common knowledge</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> effect where everyone expects their comments to be read by everyone else. This makes posting less scary. This also means the site can host more content whilst also having more posts, as your content is forced upon everyone, so </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/jkaufman?from=post_header" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">jefftk</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> can happily crosspost all his posts about his kids and contra dance, and Inkhaven Residents like Dean, Jenn, KatjaGrace, and myself, can post daily for a month without needing every post to be "ready for primetime".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We do still have curated posts, which go to the top of everyone's feed, normally like 5 days after being published. It doesn't quite feel the same as old LessWrong in that way. Perhaps it is another way to add to this shared conversation, to encourage authors to comment on the Curated Posts more.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Click to expand and read the HN and LW sorting algorithm.</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also this isn't necessary but I enjoy talking about it: The LessWrong 2.0 algorithm is inspired by </span><a href="https://github.com/wting/hackernews/blob/master/news.arc" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the Hacker News algorithm</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which ranks posts proportional to their points and recency. Here's an approximation.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6y741yjpq3e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6y741yjpq3e">[1]</a></sup></span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And here's an approximation of the LessWrong 2.0 algorithm.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefds450o51b1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnds450o51b1">[2]</a></sup></span></p><div></div></div></details><p><b><strong>Back to the main thread. </strong></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I greatly appreciate the work you did generating the table, let me recreate it here as a series of charts that are immediately impactful to the eye.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795545824/lexical_client_uploads/iyw8foxuulqmsmnpqrkw.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The natural conclusion of your post is as follows.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think that anyone who's interested in revitalizing LessWrong should start with the comment section, particularly top authors. Reply to posts, and reply to comments, especially by authors with less standing than you. Treat authors here like they have something worth thinking about. Act like there might be a conversation worth having in the comment section, and write comments that stand a chance of eliciting it.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a good idea, and I will scheme to make it happen more. (I think this is part of why the Quick Takes feature has been feeling somewhat more lively to me, as a place where people are reliably commenting on one another's writing.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But I actually have an alternative proposal.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For thinking about the internet-of-writers, I found it valuable to read Chapter 48 of Ada Palmer's "</span><a href="https://www.amazon.com/dp/1035910136?plpRedirect=mhFallback&amp;ref=clp_hp_h_pc&amp;th=1" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Inventing the Renaissance"</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, about how the early Republic of Letters worked during the Enlightenment Era. It is more blogosphere-like than I had realized! Here's the key quote.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Note: "umanisti" is a scholar or teacher of the old </span><i><em>humanities</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Letters were core to </span><i><em>umanisti</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, not the quick personal notes asking family to send fresh laundry, but literary works, polished, elaborate, modeled on Pliny or Cicero, often expounding on some virtue (patience, peace), or on some passage (a line from Propertius, or a newly discovered inscription)... Such letters were also meant to be collected and published, so to be the addressee was to have your name preserved forever in a great scholar’s </span><i><em>opera</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. To receive such a letter was to be acknowledged as a node in the learned web, and to have your name spread far and wide, in life and for posterity. And, like any social medium, this world of letters had its share of nastiness, as recorded in a Ficino letter to Bernardo Rucellai advising him not to respond to </span><s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">internet trolls</span></s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> detractors, saying such a dog will keep barking until it dies, unless it joins Cerberus in barking even after death.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In such a world, a young scholar hoping to enter the humanist world often made his debut (or tried to) by writing a learned letter to some famous figure, discussing a passage in the figure’s work or lines from a classic that the figure edited, quoting as many sources as possible to display erudition. It was your debut humanist gymnastics routine. If the famous man </span><i><em>replied</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, if you got a letter </span><i><em>back</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from Erasmus, Pontano, Poliziano, etc., if he thought </span><i><em>your</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> ideas were worth discussing, you were </span><i><em>in</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Others would write to you, scholars who passed your town would stop to visit, you could ask them for letters of recommendation, career paths opened, you were </span><i><em>somebody</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This wasn’t just academic fame; </span><i><em>umanisti</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> were celebrities, and a whole town would hear the news (and often the read-aloud letter) if someone local got a letter from a major scholar, or even if someone carrying such a letter came through town. Petrarch used to complain that people were so eager for his letters that they were copied numerous times en route, and read by many before they reached the addressee.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Thanks to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/jenn?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@jenn</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for the recommendation and for making the connection to blogging e.g. with all of our contra-posts.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What do I like about this? Well, I have always felt that it is virtuous to speak in the public sphere as close to how you would in private, rather than pretending to dialogue but actually just playing for the crowd. This lets the audience see your real cruxes and real perspective, rather than one you have designed for them that may be oversimplified or optimize for a desired outcome rather than for accuracy.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So I find that this is good writing advice. Furthermore, Pulitzer Prizewinner John McPhee </span><a href="https://www.newyorker.com/magazine/2013/04/29/draft-no-4" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">recommends</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> writing to an individual as a good way to do writing generally.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Dear Joel: You are writing, say, about a grizzly bear. No words are forthcoming. For six, seven, ten hours no words have been forthcoming. You are blocked, frustrated, in despair. You are nowhere, and that’s where you’ve been getting. What do you do? You write, ‘Dear Mother.’ And then you tell your mother about the block, the frustration, the ineptitude, the despair. You insist that you are not cut out to do this kind of work. You whine. You whimper. You outline your problem, and you mention that the bear has a fifty-five-inch waist and a neck more than thirty inches around but could run nose-to-nose with Secretariat. You say the bear prefers to lie down and rest. The bear rests fourteen hours a day. And you go on like that as long as you can. And then you go back and delete the ‘Dear Mother’ and all the whimpering and whining, and just keep the bear.”</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I make the radical observation</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that we do not need to cut "Dear Mother" if in fact we are writing letters to others. Yes, great public dialogue often </span><i><em>considers</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the audience. In my opinion the best podcasts are real conversations between experts, not performative ones. It also is truly exciting to people who are interested. Witnessing a research conversation between Von Neumann and Kurt Gödel has the potential to be far more thrilling and inspiring to a young mathematician than reading a lecturer pitch them on going to grad school.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The comment section under a post is often not for the purpose of talking to the author, but for talking to the many other commenters. Most replies to comments on posts aren't from the author, but from others, and so you are mostly not in dialogue with the author. This can lead to a perverse dynamic where you write </span><i><em>as though</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> you are speaking to the author, but in your heart you know you are speaking to the commenters and do not really intend for the author as your primary audience; the direct address is a pretense.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Aside: It sounds here as though I am recommending that all authors ought to reply to most comments on their post. I don't believe this so strongly. I will say that I think it has kept the lifeblood in the comment sections of Shtetl-Optimized and SlateStarCodex that the author often </span><i><em>would</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> respond substantively in the comments, making it a live possibility running through each comment written by others. I also think that if people come to expect this on your posts, you will get better comments. But I think it is fine for the chatter in LessWrong comments to be substantially amongst readers; we have lived that way for a long time and still had many interesting comments.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In a recent argument about communication practices, Habryka wrote </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/N7aqTbmwFBXZkSkQQ/maxwell-love-s-shortform?commentId=pKQuEws3iwyqpJSwE" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a comment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> declaring modern "media training" as a pathological version of this. (He is replying to someone else's comment, which is why there are nested quotes.)</span></p><blockquote><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was trying to convey that it's stronger to operate in our desired frame instead of the opponent's. Explaining feels like it can slide into a defensive posture. </span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think this very instinct is probably at the core of like 50% of the dysfunction of modern discourse. It's just people repeatedly insisting to have everything play out in their specific frame, and repeatedly trying to redirect everything towards that. I have a similar deep distaste for what people like to call "media training" the central lesson of which is to "not answer any question asked straightforwardly but instead use it as a jumping off point for talking about the things you want to talk about". "Media trained" people are obnoxious to talk to, and make communication and understanding of an issue reliably worse.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the goal is public persuasion, I'd redirect to what we want people talking about, ideally going on the offensive</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No, this is bad. Talk about what will help people understand the situation better. If you need to redirect, first answer the question that is being asked of you straightforwardly, even if it seems to you in bad faith, then explain something in return that you think is relevant. Conversation, including public conversation, is a give and take of different perspectives.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, I have put together the idea that we should be responding to each other in public, and have genuine dialogue. How exactly shall we go about this? I think your take that we should be writing each other comments is good. But here I would like to go further, and propose that we should be writing each other posts! And the responses should be posts as well! Do not shy away from a post.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have often felt that many substantive comments are only a little bit of effort away from being posts. A post is a much more useful contribution to the discourse. A title makes it last in memory, and also searchable on Google. LessWrong has a feature where if post X links to post Y, then post X is shown at the bottom of post Y in a section called "Mentioned in".</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 87.16012084592145%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795580203/lexical_client_uploads/e1n1o5jhy2ovyfn9wgbm.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It takes a bit of cleaning up to make the content something that you intend for people to have a good reading experience with, and to flow naturally. But the reach increases, and it is itself extending the conversation more (in that it invites a new round of comments).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have also felt that many thoughtful writers' responses to posts </span><i><em>should</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be posts, rather than comments. It feels inappropriate to write many thousands of words in a comment box, so people largely don't (</span><a href="https://It would be helpful to know to what extent Paul feels like he endorses the FAQ here. This makes it sound like Yet Another Stab At Boiling Down The Disagreement would say that I disagree with Paul on two critical points:(1) To what extent &quot;using gradient descent or anything like it to do supervised learning&quot; involves a huge amount of Project Chaos and Software Despair before things get straightened out, if they ever do;(2) Whether there's a simple scalable core to corrigibility that you can find by searching for thought processes that seem to be corrigible over relatively short ranges of scale.I don't want to invest huge amounts arguing with this until I know to what extent Paul agrees with either the FAQ, or that this sounds like a plausible locus of disagreement. But a gloss on my guess at the disagreement might be:1:Paul thinks that current ML methods given a ton more computing power will suffice to give us a basically neutral, not of itself ill-motivated, way of producing better conformance of a function to an input-output behavior implied by labeled data, which can learn things on the order of complexity of &quot;corrigible behavior&quot; and do so without containing tons of weird squiggles; Paul thinks you can iron out the difference between &quot;mostly does what you want&quot; and &quot;very exact reproduction of what you want&quot; by using more power within reasonable bounds of the computing power that might be available to a large project in N years when AGI is imminent, or through some kind of weird recursion. Paul thinks you do not get Project Chaos and Software Despair that takes more than 6 months to iron out when you try to do this. Eliezer thinks that in the alternate world where this is true, GANs pretty much worked the first time they were tried, and research got to very stable and robust behavior that boiled down to having no discernible departures from &quot;reproduce the target distribution as best you can&quot; within 6 months of being invented.Eliezer expects great Project Chaos and Software Despair from trying to use gradient descent, genetic algorithms, or anything like that, as the basic optimization to reproduce par-human cognition within a boundary in great fidelity to that boundary as the boundary was implied by human-labeled data. Eliezer thinks that if you have any optimization powerful enough to reproduce humanlike cognition inside a detailed boundary by looking at a human-labeled dataset trying to outline the boundary, the thing doing the optimization is powerful enough that we cannot assume its neutrality the way we can assume the neutrality of gradient descent.Eliezer expects weird squiggles from gradient descent - it's not that gradient descent can never produce par-human cognition, even natural selection will do that if you dump in enough computing power. But you will get the kind of weird squiggles in the learned function that adversarial examples expose in current nets - special inputs that weren't in the training distribution, but look like typical members of the training distribution from the perspective of the training distribution itself, will break what we think is the intended labeling from outside the system. Eliezer does not think Ian Goodfellow will have created a competitive form of supervised learning by gradient descent which lacks &quot;squiggles&quot; findable by powerful intelligence by the time anyone is trying to create ML-based AGI, though Eliezer is certainly cheering Goodfellow on about this and would recommend allocating Goodfellow $1 billion if Goodfellow said he could productively use it. You cannot iron out the squiggles just by using more computing power in bounded in-universe amounts.These squiggles in the learned function could correspond to daemons, if they grow large enough, or just something that breaks our hoped-for behavior from outside the system when the system is put under a load of optimization. In general, Eliezer thinks that if you have scaled up ML to produce or implement some components of an Artificial General Intelligence, those components do not have a behavior that looks like &quot;We put in loss function L, and we got out something that really actually minimizes L&quot;. You get something that minimizes some of L and has weird squiggles around typical-looking inputs (inputs not obviously distinguished from the training distribution except insofar as they exploit squiggles). The system is subjecting itself to powerful optimization that produces unusual inputs and weird execution trajectories - any output that accomplishes the goal is weird compared to a random output and it may have other weird properties as well. You can't just assume you can train for X in a robust way when you have a loss function that targets X.I imagine that Paul replies to this saying &quot;I agree, but...&quot; but I'm not sure what comes after the &quot;but&quot;. It looks to me like Paul is imagining that you can get very powerful optimization with very detailed conformance to our intended interpretation of the dataset, powerful enough to enclose par-human cognition inside a boundary drawn from human labeling of a dataset, and have that be the actual thing we get out rather than a weird thing full of squiggles. If Paul thinks he has a way to compound large conformant recursive systems out of par-human thingies that start out weird and full of squiggles, we should definitely be talking about that. From my perspective it seems like Paul repeatedly reasons &quot;We train for X and get X&quot; rather than &quot;We train for X and get something that mostly conforms to X but has a bunch of weird squiggles&quot; and also often speaks as if the training method is assumed to be gradient descent, genetic algorithms, or something else that can be assumed neutral-of-itself rather than being an-AGI-of-itself whose previous alignment has to be assumed.The imaginary Paul in my head replies that we actually are using an AGI to train on X and get X, but this AGI was previously trained by a weaker neutral AGI, and so on going back to something trained by gradient descent. My imaginary reply is that neutrality is not the same property as conformance or nonsquiggliness, and if you train your base AGI via neutral gradient descent you get out a squiggly AGI and this squiggly AGI is not neutral when it comes to that AGI looking at a dataset produced by X and learning a function conformant to X. Or to put it another way, if the plan is to use gradient descent on human-labeled data to produce a corrigible alien that is smart enough to produce more corrigible aliens better than gradient descent, this corrigible alien actually needs to be quite smart because an IQ 100 human will not build an aligned IQ 140 human even if you run them for a thousand years, so you are producing something very smart and dangerous on the first step, and gradient descent is not smart enough to align that base case.But at this point I expect the real Paul to come back and say, &quot;No, no, the idea is something else...&quot;A very important aspect of my objection to Paul here is that I don't expect weird complicated ideas about recursion to work on the first try, with only six months of additional serial labor put into stabilizing them, which I understand to be Paul's plan. In the world where you can build a weird recursive stack of neutral optimizers into conformant behavioral learning on the first try, GANs worked on the first try too, because that world is one whose general Murphy parameter is set much lower than ours. Being able to build weird recursive stacks of optimizers that work correctly to produce neutral and faithful optimization for corrigible superhuman thought out of human-labeled corrigible behaviors and corrigible reasoning, without very much of a time penalty relative to nearly-equally-resourced projects who are just cheerfully revving all the engines as hard as possible trying to destroy the world, is just not how things work in real life, dammit. Even if you could make the weird recursion work, it would take time.2:Eliezer thinks that while corrigibility probably has a core which is of lower algorithmic complexity than all of human value, this core is liable to be very hard to find or reproduce by supervised learning of human-labeled data, because deference is an unusually anti-natural shape for cognition, in a way that a simple utility function would not be an anti-natural shape for cognition. Utility functions have multiple fixpoints requiring the infusion of non-environmental data, our externally desired choice of utility function would be non-natural in that sense, but that's not what we're talking about, we're talking about anti-natural behavior.E.g.: Eliezer also thinks that there is a simple core describing a reflective superintelligence which believes that 51 is a prime number, and actually behaves like that including when the behavior incurs losses, and doesn't thereby ever promote the hypothesis that 51 is not prime or learn to safely fence away the cognitive consequences of that belief and goes on behaving like 51 is a prime number, while having no other outwardly discernible deficits of cognition except those that directly have to do with 51. Eliezer expects there's a relatively simple core for that, a fixed point of tangible but restrained insanity that persists in the face of scaling and reflection; there's a relatively simple superintelligence that refuses to learn around this hole, refuses to learn how to learn around this hole, refuses to fix itself, but is otherwise capable of self-improvement and growth and reflection, etcetera. But the core here has a very anti-natural shape and you would be swimming uphill hard if you tried to produce that core in an indefinitely scalable way that persisted under reflection. You would be very unlikely to get there by training really hard on a dataset where humans had labeled as the 'correct' behavior what humans thought would be the implied behavior if 51 were a prime number, not least because gradient descent is terrible, but also just because you'd be trying to lift 10 pounds of weirdness with an ounce of understanding.The central reasoning behind this intuition of anti-naturalness is roughly, &quot;Non-deference converges really hard as a consequence of almost any detailed shape that cognition can take&quot;, with a side order of &quot;categories over behavior that don't simply reduce to utility functions or meta-utility functions are hard to make robustly scalable&quot;.The real reasons behind this intuition are not trivial to pump, as one would expect of an intuition that Paul Christiano has been alleged to have not immediately understood. A couple of small pumps would be https://arbital.com/p/updated_deference/ for the first intuition and https://arbital.com/p/expected_utility_formalism/?l=7hh for the second intuition.What I imagine Paul is imagining is that it seems to him like it would in some sense be not that hard for a human who wanted to be very corrigible toward an alien, to be very corrigible toward that alien; so you ought to be able to use gradient-descent-class technology to produce a base-case alien that wants to be very corrigible to us, the same way that natural selection sculpted humans to have a bunch of other desires, and then you apply induction on it building more corrigible things.My class of objections in (1) is that natural selection was actually selecting for inclusive fitness when it got us, so much for going from the loss function to the cognition; and I have problems with both the base case and the induction step of what I imagine to be Paul's concept of solving this using recursive optimization bootstrapping itself; and even more so do I have trouble imagining it working on the first, second, or tenth try over the course of the first six months.My class of objections in (2) is that it's not a coincidence that humans didn't end up deferring to natural selection, or that in real life if we were faced with a very bizarre alien we would be unlikely to want to defer to it. Our lack of scalable desire to defer in all ways to an extremely bizarre alien that ate babies, is not something that you could fix just by giving us an emotion of great deference or respect toward that very bizarre alien. We would have our own thought processes that were unlike its thought processes, and if we scaled up our intelligence and reflection to further see the consequences implied by our own thought processes, they wouldn't imply deference to the alien even if we had great respect toward it and had been trained hard in childhood to act corrigibly towards it.A dangerous intuition pump here would be something like, &quot;If you take a human who was trained really hard in childhood to have faith in God and show epistemic deference to the Bible, and inspecting the internal contents of their thought at age 20 showed that they still had great faith, if you kept amping up that human's intelligence their epistemology would at some point explode&quot;; and this is true even though it's other humans training the human, and it's true even though religion as a weird sticking point of human thought is one we selected post-hoc from the category of things historically proven to be tarpits of human psychology, rather than aliens trying from the outside in advance to invent something that would stick the way religion sticks. I use this analogy with some reluctance because of the clueless readers who will try to map it onto the AGI losing religious faith in the human operators, which is not what this analogy is about at all; the analogy here is about the epistemology exploding as you ramp up intelligence because the previous epistemology had a weird shape.Acting corrigibly towards a baby-eating virtue ethicist when you are a utilitarian is an equally weird shape for a decision theory. It probably does have a fixed point but it's not an easy one, the same way that &quot;yep, on reflection and after a great deal of rewriting my own thought processes, I sure do still think that 51 is prime&quot; probably has a fixed point but it's not an easy one.I think I can imagine an IQ 100 human who defers to baby-eating aliens, although I really think a lot of this is us post-hoc knowing that certain types of thoughts can be sticky, rather than the baby-eating aliens successfully guessing in advance how religious faith works for humans and training the human to think that way using labeled data.But if you ramp up the human's intelligence to where they are discovering subjective expected utility and logical decision theory and they have an exact model of how the baby-eating aliens work and they are rewriting their own minds, it's harder to imagine the shape of deferential thought at IQ 100 successfully scaling to a shape of deferential thought at IQ 1000.Eliezer also tends to be very skeptical of attempts to cross cognitive chasms between A and Z by going through weird recursions and inductive processes that wouldn't work equally well to go directly from A to Z. http://slatestarcodex.com/2014/10/12/five-planets-in-search-of-a-sci-fi-story/ and the story of K'th'ranga V is a good intuition pump here. So Eliezer is also not very hopeful that Paul will come up with a weirdly recursive solution that scales deference to IQ 101, IQ 102, etcetera, via deferential agents building other deferential agents, in a way that Eliezer finds persuasive. Especially a solution that works on merely the tenth try over the first six months, doesn't kill you when the first nine tries fail, and doesn't require more than 10x extra computing power compared to projects that are just bulling cheerfully ahead.3:I think I have a disagreement with Paul about the notion of being able to expose inspectable thought processes to humans, such that we can examine each step of the thought process locally and determine whether it locally has properties that will globally add up to corrigibility, alignment, and intelligence. It's not that I think this can never be done, or even that I think it takes longer than six months. In this case, I think this problem is literally isomorphic to &quot;build an aligned AGI&quot;. If you can locally inspect cognitive steps for properties that globally add to intelligence, corrigibility, and alignment, you're done; you've solved the AGI alignment problem and you can just apply the same knowledge to directly build an aligned corrigible intelligence.As I currently flailingly attempt to understand Paul, Paul thinks that having humans do the inspection (base case) or thingies trained to resemble aggregates of trained thingies (induction step) is something we can do in an intuitive sense by inspecting a reasoning step and seeing if it sounds all aligned and corrigible and intelligent. Eliezer thinks that the large-scale or macro traces of cognition, e.g. a &quot;verbal stream of consciousness&quot; or written debates, are not complete with respect to general intelligence in bounded quantities; we are generally intelligent because of sub-verbal cognition whose intelligence-making properties are not transparent to inspection. That is: An IQ 100 person who can reason out loud about Go, but who can't learn from the experience of playing Go, is not a complete general intelligence over boundedly reasonable amounts of reasoning time.This means you have to be able to inspect steps like &quot;learn an intuition for Go by playing Go&quot; for local properties that will globally add to corrigible aligned intelligence. And at this point it no longer seems intuitive that having humans do the inspection is adding a lot of value compared to us directly writing a system that has the property.This is a previous discussion that is ongoing between Paul and myself, and I think it's a crux of disagreement but not one that's as cruxy as 1 and 2. Although it might be a subcrux of my belief that you can't use weird recursion starting from gradient descent on human-labeled data to build corrigible agents that build corrigible agents. I think Paul is modeling the grain size here as corrigible thoughts rather than whole agents, which if it were a sensible way to think, might make the problem look much more manageable; but I don't think you can build corrigible thoughts without building corrigible agents to think them unless you have solved the decomposition problem that I think is isomorphic to building an aligned corrigible intelligence directly.I remark that this intuition matches what the wise might learn from Scott's parable of K'th'ranga V: If you know how to do something then you know how to do it directly rather than by weird recursion, and what you imagine yourself doing by weird recursion you probably can't really do at all. When you want an airplane you don't obtain it by figuring out how to build birds and then aggregating lots of birds into a platform that can carry more weight than any one bird and then aggregating platforms into megaplatforms until you have an airplane; either you understand aerodynamics well enough to build an airplane, or you don't, the weird recursion isn't really doing the work. It is by no means clear that we would have a superior government free of exploitative politicians if all the voters elected representatives whom they believed to be only slightly smarter than themselves, until a chain of delegation reached up to the top level of government; either you know how to build a less corruptible relationship between voters and politicians, or you don't, the weirdly recursive part doesn't really help. It is no coincidence that modern ML systems do not work by weird recursion because all the discoveries are of how to just do stuff, not how to do stuff using weird recursion. (Even with AlphaGo which is arguably recursive if you squint at it hard enough, you're looking at something that is not weirdly recursive the way I think Paul's stuff is weirdly recursive, and for more on that see https://intelligence.org/2018/05/19/challenges-to-christianos-capability-amplification-proposal/.)It's in this same sense that I intuit that if you could inspect the local elements of a modular system for properties that globally added to aligned corrigible intelligence, it would mean you had the knowledge to build an aligned corrigible AGI out of parts that worked like that, not that you could aggregate systems that corrigibly learned to put together sequences of corrigible thoughts into larger corrigible thoughts starting from gradient descent on data humans have labeled with their own judgments of corrigibility." rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">counterexample!</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), but if they opened the new post editor, they would no longer feel this constraint. (I certainly wouldn't have written all of this in a comment!)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One format that I have been experimenting with lately, is letters. Perhaps even using lines like "My dearest Directed Evolution" and so on. A few days ago I got to chatting with </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/daystareld?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@DaystarEld</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> at Inkhaven, where he asked if I'd read his </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dp3D39ztWDWFAN6nu/three-villages-in-a-trenchcoat-post-i"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Three Villages in a Trenchcoat, Post I</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I replied I had not, but when I did that night, I was quite surprised by the quantity and quality of the insights, and soon </span><a href="https://benpace.blog/blogpost-letter-to-daystar.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wrote him a letter</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that I hosted on my blog. (The style is a little romantic, I have been experimenting much visually during </span><a href="https://benpace.blog/nights.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">my Inkhaven posts</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.)</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795570934/lexical_client_uploads/w3rhukfg9y0yvpgtyt2s.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I found it a much more generative writing prompt than to simply write a post on the same subject, and I would like to do it more, and with other writers. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I bet it would be a generative experiment for many writers to pick something good they read in the last ~6 months by another writer on LessWrong, and write a letter to them to add their own thoughts on the subject. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Perhaps it will take off, and in the future when they write the history of LessWrong, they'll date this as the start of LessWrong 3.0, when the republic of letters began once more!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This I desire.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yours truthfully,</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—Ben Pace</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6y741yjpq3e"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6y741yjpq3e">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The penalties on HN are for text posts or image posts rather than link posts, certain domains, and whether the comments have spawned a demon thread of over 20 replies.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnds450o51b1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefds450o51b1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The bonuses are primarily the tag bonuses (or penalties) people apply through their tag filters, and also a +10 if a post is curated. The gamma term is proportional to </span><i><em>how often someone visits LessWrong</em></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, punishing older posts if someone visits daily or more.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RjydrDjY8sgWy2rav/a-republic-of-lesswrong-letters-a-letter-to#comments">Discuss</a>
